And there is danger in it: when they are not repaired, they kill the person. So I do not want to eat." On Friday morning he ordered that they give him food, and he ate.

After the meal he called the rabbi aside, and said: "Know that it was revealed to me from Heaven that in the villages around the town there are two teachers who have a share in these sparks that I must repair. And Heaven has made it seem to them that they are ill, so that they will come to the town to be healed. So station your servant at the town gate, to bring them to you. Have pity on them, but do not show them any honor at all. And let your honor promise them that he will speak for them before me, so that I will heal them. And persuade them to go to the mikveh, for since it seems to them that they are ill, they will not want to go to the mikveh. And tell them that I like it when people come before me pure from the mikveh. And lend them shirts for the Sabbath, and long coats, for they are poor. Let them eat on the Sabbath at a householder's, and not at your honor's. And when they come to the study house, even though I will be standing in the Amidah, whisper in my ear that they have come." And so they did.

After the meal the teachers came to the rabbi's house, and they did not give them even a small glass of mead, and so too in the morning. And at the third Sabbath meal they ate there, and were given mead. After Havdalah the Baal Shem Tov was filled with joy, took his pipe, and went to the small house where he was staying. The rabbi wanted to go after him too, and the two teachers came, and asked the rabbi to bring them in to the Baal Shem Tov. He said to them: "Come with me." And they went in with the rabbi. The Baal Shem Tov said to the rabbi: "They do not need healing. Let them be given the shirts and the long coats as a gift, and let them go in peace." And the teachers knew nothing.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov fasted for several days in the town of the Rabbi of Polonne, for there were sparks there that were hard to repair. Heaven caused two poor teachers, who had a share in the sparks, to think they were ill and come to the town, and the Baal Shem Tov told the rabbi exactly how to receive them: to send them to the mikveh, to clothe them for the Sabbath, and not to honor them. At the end of the Sabbath the Baal Shem Tov was joyful, and said that they did not need healing and should be given the clothes as a gift, and the teachers knew nothing.

## 66 — The Light of Rabbi Shimshon in Anipoli

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov did not want to be in the town of Anipoli, for he said: "I have no business there, for Rabbi Shimshon of that town has already repaired there everything that needed repairing." Once his brother-in-law, Rabbi Gershon, spent the Sabbath there, and came from there and said to the Baal Shem Tov: "I perceived a great light there from Rabbi Shimshon." He said to him: "You are deceiving yourself."

Once they sent for the Baal Shem Tov to come to Anipoli. During the meal he sat facing the window, opened the window, and looked through it every moment. Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka wondered at this, and wanted to know why he was looking out of the window every moment. What did he do? When the Baal Shem Tov turned away from the window, he shut it. When the Baal Shem Tov wanted to look, he opened the window, and again Rabbi Yosef shut it. And he did so a third time too, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and said: "Who is it who keeps shutting the window?" Rabbi Yosef said: "I am, for the cold comes into the house through the window. And why do you open it every moment?" And the Baal Shem Tov said: "Do you not see how the pillar of Torah of Rabbi Shimshon shines from the earth to the sky?" (And they told me that at that time there was a rubbish heap in that place, and they said that Rabbi Shimshon's study house had stood in that place.) And he said: "Today I learned what Rabbi Gershon said."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov did not want to come to Anipoli, for Rabbi Shimshon had already repaired everything there, and when Rabbi Gershon told him he had seen a great light there from Rabbi Shimshon, he told him he was deceiving himself. When the Baal Shem Tov finally came to Anipoli, he opened the window again and again during the meal, although Rabbi Yosef shut it because of the cold. He explained that he saw a pillar of Torah of Rabbi Shimshon shining from the earth to the sky, and admitted that Rabbi Gershon had been right.

## 67 — The Tollkeeper Whose Son Was Imprisoned

I heard that there was a tollkeeper near Mezhibuzh. Another man came and offered the nobleman more money for the lease, and the tollkeeper was driven out of the village. He was left owing money to the nobleman of the village, and he had nothing to pay with. The nobleman, and the new tollkeeper too, gave orders that he must not be allowed to leave the village for anywhere. Even so, the former tollkeeper traveled to the Baal Shem Tov. When the new tollkeeper found out, he informed on him to the nobleman and said: "He has gone to a sorcerer, to put a spell on you." The nobleman was angry with him, and took his son into prison.

The tollkeeper did not find the Baal Shem Tov at home, for he was in the town of Kaminka. He traveled after him to the town, and did not find him there, for the Baal Shem Tov had gone home. He traveled after him, and caught up with him on the road. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "What can I do for you on the road? Follow me to my house." On the way the Baal Shem Tov saw that the nobleman had taken the tollkeeper's son into prison. He said: "There is no time to wait." They came to a river, and the Baal Shem Tov immersed himself in it. When he came out of the river, he said to the tollkeeper: "Go home, and as soon as you reach the village, go to the castle." And he did so. When the nobleman saw him from the window, he was afraid he would harm him with his witchcraft, and he released his son from prison, and sent his servant to drive him out of the village with everything he had.

Afterward the nobleman sent a letter to the commissar of Mezhibuzh, with a great complaint against the Baal Shem Tov, and wrote this: "How can you keep a sorcerer in your town?" And the commissar answered him: "Make every effort to win the Baal Shem Tov over, for he is not a sorcerer. And if you do not win him over, you will be punished from Heaven." And at once the nobleman sent to make peace with him, and sent him a gift: a wagon with all kinds of flour and poultry.

**Summary:** A tollkeeper who had been driven out of his village and was left in debt to the nobleman traveled in secret to the Baal Shem Tov, and the new tollkeeper informed that he had gone to a sorcerer, and the nobleman imprisoned his son. The Baal Shem Tov immersed himself in a river and sent the tollkeeper straight to the castle, and the nobleman took fright, released the son and drove the family out of the village. When the nobleman complained to the commissar of Mezhibuzh that he was keeping a sorcerer, the commissar answered that the Baal Shem Tov was not a sorcerer and that he must make peace with him, and the nobleman sent the Baal Shem Tov a wagon full of gifts.

## 68 — How the Baal Shem Tov Learned to Speak with People

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne that the Baal Shem Tov could not speak with people, because of his attachment to God, and would say things that did not fit the matter at hand. His master, the well-known one, taught him to say every day the psalm "Happy are those whose way is blameless," and several other particular psalms, and showed him wisdom. And through this he began to speak with people, and his attachment was not lost. And he would say these psalms every day.

**Summary:** Because of his attachment to God, the Baal Shem Tov could not speak properly with people. His master taught him to say particular psalms every day, and through them he began to speak with people without losing his attachment.

## 69 — The Sick Child and the Oath

I heard from Rabbi Pesach, the son of Rabbi Yaakov of Kaminka, that the Baal Shem Tov was traveling, and came to a town, and heard a proclamation that he would stay in a certain house. He came to this house, and they did not want to take him in as a guest, for the householder's child was very ill. The Baal Shem Tov sent the scribe into the house, and the woman said: "How can anyone stay here? You see the sick child, and I am in great sorrow!" And she cursed the Baal Shem Tov. The householder did not dare, and went out to the Baal Shem Tov to plead with him, and said that it was impossible. The Baal Shem Tov swore to him that if he stayed with him, the child would live. And the householder took him into his house.

At once the Baal Shem Tov went to the mikveh, and saw that the child's condition was not good. He ordered that no one be in that house, and they all went to another house. He also ordered the scribe not to be in that house, until he called him to give him wine for Kiddush, for it was Friday. He stayed alone with the child, and prayed the afternoon prayer beside the child, and stayed there a long time, until well into the night. The scribe was afraid that the Baal Shem Tov might be in danger, Heaven forbid, because of his great effort in praying for the sick child, for that is a dangerous thing. The scribe went, and opened the door quietly, and heard the Baal Shem Tov saying to the child's soul: "Go into the body! For you will be forced to go in, for I will not swear falsely." The scribe did not know whether the child had died, or was still alive, with a little life still in him.

The scribe went away, and after a short time came back in, and found the Baal Shem Tov lying on the ground, his hands and feet stretched out. The Baal Shem Tov got up and said: "I told you to go into the child's body!" And he called out: "Hirsh, give wine for Kiddush!" He ate with the scribe, and did not sleep all night. In the morning he gave the scribe the medicines and the instructions, and went to pray in the study house. The child's mother understood that the child had come back to his strength, and began to weep bitterly. The scribe heard, and asked her: "Why are you weeping?" She said to him: "How can I not weep, when I cursed such a tzaddik?" He answered her: "Do not weep. My master is a good man, and he will forgive you." When the Baal Shem Tov came from the prayer, he too heard her weeping. He asked the scribe, and the scribe told him. The Baal Shem Tov sent the scribe to her, and said: "Tell her not to weep, and to make a good third Sabbath meal, and I promise her that the child will sit with us at the table at that meal."

And as for the Baal Shem Tov falling to the ground, his hands and feet stretched out: it was because he took upon himself to receive *pulsa denura* (lashes of fire in Heaven) because of the oath, and the soul was forced to go into the body. And he brought it about that the child would live more than sixty years, and have children and a good living all the days of his life. And from this one sees that the child's end had come, and so he had to pray for how many years he would live, and for a living and for children.

**Summary:** In a certain town the Baal Shem Tov heard a proclamation that he should stay in a house where a very sick child lay, and the mother cursed him, but the Baal Shem Tov swore to the father that if he stayed with him the child would live. He stayed alone with the child and prayed until night, ordered the child's soul to go into the body, and took upon himself a harsh punishment in Heaven so that the oath would not be false. The child recovered, the mother wept for having cursed a tzaddik, and the Baal Shem Tov promised that the child would sit with them at the third meal, and he lived more than sixty years.

## 70 — The Tree That Burned in the Forest

Rabbi Pesach also told me: once the Baal Shem Tov traveled from Kaminka to his home, and Rabbi Baruch of Kaminka traveled with him, in a separate wagon. The scribe sat with the Baal Shem Tov, and the flour wagon traveled behind him too. The Baal Shem Tov said: "At such-and-such a place we will pray the afternoon prayer." It was in the winter, and it was very cold. That place was several parsahs away, and they were frozen through, and the cold got into their bodies. They said: "It is impossible to reach that place, for it is still far, and the cold will overcome all of us." And the Baal Shem Tov's servant too said that he was very cold. They were traveling through a forest, and the Baal Shem Tov ordered them to stop. He touched a tree with his finger, and the tree caught fire and burned, and they warmed themselves by the fire. The servant too took off his boots, and dried the wrappings on his feet. Afterward they traveled on to that place. And as they drove away from the place, Rabbi Baruch wanted to look and see what would become of the tree, and the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him not to look.

**Summary:** On a journey in a bitterly cold winter, when everyone was frozen and there was no chance of reaching the place of prayer, the Baal Shem Tov stopped in a forest and touched a tree with his finger, and the tree burned. Everyone warmed themselves by the fire, and afterward they went on their way. When Rabbi Baruch wanted to look and see what would become of the tree, the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him not to look.

## 71 — Rabbi Shmeril's Son

I heard from Rabbi Falk HaKohen the rabbi that his father-in-law, Rabbi Shmeril, had no son. The Baal Shem Tov used to stay with him. Rabbi Shmeril had no special house for him, so the Baal Shem Tov stayed in his own house, and the women moved from there to the neighbors, and cooked there, and at mealtime they brought the food here. Once, on a Sunday, before the Baal Shem Tov left, Rabbi Shmeril wanted to ask him to pray for him to have a son. He went around and around him, and could not speak from great shame. The next day, before the journey, he again went around him to speak with him, and could not, and was ashamed before him. Suddenly the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Send for your wife." She came, and the Baal Shem Tov took his stick in his hand, and said to her: "See that you give birth to a son, or else I will break your bones with this stick!" And so it was: that year she gave birth to a son, and his name was Rabbi David, who was later head of the rabbinical court in Tetchelnik.

Rabbi Shmeril's custom was that even when his wife gave birth to a daughter, he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov, took an amulet from him, and gave him two zlotys or more, for at that time he was a merchant, and had about three thousand zlotys. When this son was born to him, he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov to take an amulet from him, as was his custom. He asked him: "Did your wife give birth to a son?" He said to him: "Yes." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Know that you will have no more money. For you were given a choice, either money or a son, and I chose for you that it is better for you to have a son." And so it was: every piece of merchandise he bought, he lost on it, until he had lost all his money. Afterward he was taken on as preacher in the town of Verkhivka. And when I knew him, when he was often with the rabbi of Kosov, he was then the authority on Jewish law in the town of Sagran.

**Summary:** Rabbi Shmeril, with whom the Baal Shem Tov used to stay, was ashamed to ask him to pray that a son be born to him, and the Baal Shem Tov himself called his wife and "threatened" her with his stick that she must give birth to a son, and that year a son was born to them. When Rabbi Shmeril came as usual to take an amulet, the Baal Shem Tov told him that Heaven had given him a choice between money and a son, and that the Baal Shem Tov had chosen the son for him. And indeed Rabbi Shmeril lost all his money, and became a preacher.

## 72 — The Dog and the Fish

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne and from the rabbi of our community that Rabbi Nachman of Kosov had a relative named Rabbi Yudel of Chudnov, and his father, Rabbi Yosef, was a preacher of reproof. Rabbi Yudel's way was not to benefit from what belonged to others, but only from the labor of his own hands, and his trade was in iron. Once he traveled to a *rudnia* (an iron-mining village), and wanted to spend the Sabbath there. The householder said: "And what will your honor eat? Fish I do not have, and meat he will not eat, for he has not checked the knife. So my advice is this: near here there is another rudnia, and a rich man lives there, and surely he has fish and a shochet. Let your honor travel to him."

He traveled, and when he reached the water channel near that rudnia (the road went through the water channel, for the water was not deep, but now there had been heavy rains, or it was the time of the melting of the snows, and the channel had filled up), they did not realize it, and wanted to cross through the channel. A dog ran ahead of them into the channel, and drowned in the water. The dog went up and down in the water, and cried out bitterly, until the pious man's pity was stirred, and his eyes shed tears. He saw that it was impossible to cross the water, and went back to the first rudnia, and asked the householder to make an effort to have fish for the Sabbath. The householder went and urged the fishermen, and they fished and brought a big fish called a pike. The householder said: "I have lived in this village for several years, and such a fish has never come my way." And they made several dishes from it for him.

On Friday night Rabbi Yudel sat at the meal, sang the Sabbath songs, and fell asleep at the table. His father appeared to him in a dream and said to him: "Know that I am reincarnated in this fish. And the informer whom I persecuted in my lifetime was reincarnated in this dog that drowned. And this was his *tikkun*: that he drowned for you, my son, to save you. And I was forced to be reincarnated in this fish, because I persecuted him. And you repaired me, in that your eyes shed tears over the drowning of the dog. And now see, my son, how you eat this fish." And when Rabbi Yudel came to the Baal Shem Tov... And the Baal Shem Tov used to say that Rabbi Yudel was a reincarnation of Samuel the Prophet.

**Summary:** Rabbi Yudel, who lived only from the labor of his hands, tried to reach a village with fish for the Sabbath, and saw a dog drowning in front of him in a channel and crying out, and wept over it, and went back to the first village. There they caught a big fish for him, whose like had not been seen. At night his father appeared in a dream, and told him that he himself was reincarnated in the fish, that the dog was the informer he had persecuted in his lifetime, that the drowning had saved Rabbi Yudel, and that his tears over the dog had repaired his father's soul.

## 73 — Rabbi Yudel Dances

I heard from Rabbi Shneur, the grandson of Rabbi Nachman, that Rabbi Yudel was with Rabbi Nachman in the town of Ludmir. Rabbi Nachman built a study house there right on the water, and the bathhouse was next to the study house, and on Sabbath morning they went to the mikveh. Rabbi Nachman was very quick, and Rabbi Yudel was a little slow. While Rabbi Yudel was still taking off his clothes, Rabbi Nachman was already standing before the ark and praying. When Rabbi Yudel came out of the mikveh, he heard Rabbi Nachman singing "Majesty and faithfulness," and he was so carried away that he ran to the study house in nothing but his shirt, without his clothes, and danced there for about two hours.

**Summary:** When Rabbi Yudel came out of the mikveh on Sabbath morning, he heard Rabbi Nachman singing "Majesty and faithfulness" in the study house. He was so carried away that he ran to the study house in only his shirt, and danced there for about two hours.

## 74 — Rabbi Nachman's Sigh

I heard from my father-in-law: when Rabbi Nachman sighed in prayer, the sigh broke the heart of whoever heard it, and it seemed to him as if he had been cut in two. The Baal Shem Tov said: "As far as the places Rabbi Nachman has traveled to, they know what prayer is. And in a place where Rabbi Nachman has not been, they do not know what prayer is."

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman's sigh in prayer would break the hearts of those who heard it. The Baal Shem Tov said that in every place Rabbi Nachman had reached, they knew what prayer is, and in a place where he had not been, they did not.

## 75 — Rabbi Nachman in Zholkva

I heard: once Rabbi Nachman passed through the town of Zholkva, with merchandise, at the time of the morning prayer. He stopped with his wagon in front of the study house, took his tallit and tefillin, went into the study house, wrapped himself in his tallit and put on tefillin, and stood before the ark to lead the prayers, without permission. The people were angry: how did the guest dare to stand before the ark without permission? But when they heard the words that came out of his mouth, sweeter than honey and the honeycomb, they enjoyed it and kept silent. Even so, it angered them. After the Rabbis' Kaddish he began to say "Hodu" before "Baruch She'amar," and they were all filled with anger and fury, and meant to pull him away from the ark. But because they wanted very much to hear his words and his prayers, they could do nothing, and they went back and forth over it until he finished his prayer.

When he had finished all his prayers, all the people of the study house opened their mouths against him, and said to him: "How did your heart dare to stand before the ark without permission, and to change the order of prayer that our fathers and our fathers' fathers prayed in, who were the great men of the generation?" He answered and said: "And who said that they are in the Garden of Eden?" When they heard his answer, they were filled with even more fury against him. The author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef* was there, and he was very old, and had given his greatness to a disciple, to be preacher in his place, and his name was Rabbi Zalman. And Rabbi Zalman fell upon Rabbi Nachman more than all of them. Rabbi Alexander, Rabbi Zalman's disciple, came and said: "Leave the man alone, for he is always with God." And since we have come to this, let us hear another thing on this matter.

**Summary:** On his way Rabbi Nachman went into the study house in Zholkva, and stood up to lead the prayers before the ark without permission, and even changed the order of the prayer. The worshipers were very angry, but his prayer was so sweet that they did not interrupt him until the end. When they attacked him for changing from the order of their great fathers, he answered "And who said that they are in the Garden of Eden?" and Rabbi Alexander told them to leave him alone, for he is always with God.

## 76 — Praying in the Place of the Author of the *Toldot*

I heard from the rabbi, who used to say this: a saying often on the lips of the tzaddik Rabbi Yaakov Yosef was that it was easier for him to say ten sharp arguments in Torah than to pray one Amidah. And I heard from Rabbi Zusya of Anipoli: "When I traveled from my brother, Rabbi Elimelech, through Zholkva, I stood in a certain place in the study house to pray, and the prayer was pure and clear, like the prayers of the Baal Shem Tov. I did not know for what reason, and they told me that this place had been the place of the author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef*."

**Summary:** Rabbi Yaakov Yosef used to say that it was easier for him to make ten sharp Torah arguments than to pray one Amidah. Rabbi Zusya told that when he prayed in the study house in Zholkva, his prayer was as pure as the Baal Shem Tov's prayer, and they told him that he had stood in the place where the author of the *Toldot* used to pray.

## 77 — Elijah and His Master in the Sickroom

I heard from the rabbi of our community: when the Baal Shem Tov was ill before his passing, he did not lie in bed, but he grew thin, and his voice grew weak. He sat in his house of seclusion, and had no strength to call his servant when he needed him. So he hung a bell in the big house, and a string ran from it to his small house. The Baal Shem Tov pulled the string, and the sound of the bell was heard, and the servant came to him.

Once Rabbi David Purkes, the preacher of Mezhibuzh, came in to him and asked him something. (From a man from Bar I heard that the question was how to pray for a sick person, with stories.) The Baal Shem Tov began to answer him, and as he spoke he was greatly carried away, and his face burned like torches. Fear fell on Rabbi David, and he wanted to run away, but since the Baal Shem Tov was speaking with him face to face, he could not go out. Meanwhile his daughter, Adel, came and said to her father: "It is time to eat, for the day is already turning." And at once the redness left his face, and paleness came, and he lay down on his bed to rest. He said to her: "My daughter, my daughter, what have you done to me?" Rabbi David said: "She is right, for it is time to eat." The Baal Shem Tov said: "You did not know who was here. On one side stood Elijah the Prophet, and on the other side stood my master. They told me, and I told you. And when she came and confused me, they left." And the Baal Shem Tov said that he had taken his daughter's soul from the Torah, from the verse "*esh dat lamo*" (a fiery law for them), whose first letters spell A-D-L.

**Summary:** In the months of his illness before his passing, the Baal Shem Tov was so weak that he called his servant with a bell and a string. When Rabbi David Purkes asked him a question, the Baal Shem Tov was carried away and his face burned, until his daughter Adel came in to remind him to eat, and then he grew weak at once. He said that Elijah the Prophet and his master had been standing beside him and speaking through him, and they left when his daughter interrupted. The Baal Shem Tov said that his daughter's soul came from the verse "*esh dat lamo*," in which her name is hinted.

## 78 — Rabbi Leib, Who Made a Reckoning of His Soul

And I heard from the rabbi, who said of Rabbi Leib that he was one of the "masters of the reckoning": every day he made a reckoning of everything he had done, and so too every night. On the day he passed away, he went to the mikveh, and as he came out of the bathhouse he met the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Hurry, and do your deeds." (For his custom was to hear the "Kedushah" and the "Barchu" in all the synagogues.) He took a horse, and rode on it from synagogue to synagogue, for there was mud, and he could not go on foot. And that same day he died and passed from the world.

**Summary:** Every day and every night Rabbi Leib made a reckoning of his deeds. On the day he passed away the Baal Shem Tov told him to hurry and do his deeds, and he rode a horse through the mud from synagogue to synagogue to hear the "Kedushah" and the "Barchu," and that same day he passed away.

## 79 — The Hairs in the Chest

I heard from the rabbi of our community: in Kitov there was a man called Tektiner, and he was always speaking against the circle of the Hasidim. Once Rabbi Aaron, the Baal Shem Tov's brother-in-law, heard him and quarreled with him. The man jumped up and tore hairs out of Rabbi Aaron's beard. Rabbi Gershon, Rabbi Aaron's brother, rose and put him under a ban. A few days later that man died. And some years later Rabbi Aaron died.

Rabbi Gershon, his brother, saw him in a dream during the seven days of mourning, and heard him preaching, and enjoyed it, and ran to listen. As he came near, he recognized that it was a *kelipah* (a force of impurity). He grew angry with it and said: "Wicked one, how did your heart dare to put on the form of my brother?" And he spat at it, and it vanished from his eyes. Afterward Rabbi Leib, the Mokhiach of Polonne, also saw him during the seven days. He checked the sign, and saw that it was Rabbi Aaron himself. He asked him: "What is this, that a kelipah put on your form?" He said: "A mark of a sin remained on me, and through that mark it had the power to put on my form. May my brother, Rabbi Gershon, live long, for with his spitting he removed the mark from me."

He asked him: "How are you now?" He said to him: "In the Garden of Eden." He asked him about the man Tektiner: "What is he doing?" He said to him: "He too was in the Garden of Eden, and I did not think about him at all. When he saw me in the Garden of Eden, he said to me: 'I will sue you before the heavenly court, for because of you I died.' I was very frightened. I took the hairs, which I had wrapped in a rag and put in my chest, ran and took them, and laid them before the court. And he brought evil on himself: they drove him out of the Garden of Eden into Gehinnom."

And he asked him: "How and what are you in the Garden of Eden?" He answered: "The lower Garden of Eden is a little material. When the Sabbath eve came, I went to the synagogue. When I reached the door, I did not see any of the people I knew. I stood by the bimah, and prayed the afternoon prayer. Before the welcoming of the Sabbath a proclamation went out: 'Rabbi Aaron will lead the welcoming of the Sabbath before the ark.' I went before the ark, and said 'Come, let us sing.' And when I reached 'A psalm, a song for the Sabbath day,' they all vanished from me, and I was left alone. I did not know where they had gone, and it gave me great sorrow the whole Sabbath, that I had no company. So I ask your honor that they pray for me, so that I can go up with them next Sabbath."

The Mokhiach said to him: "You left your wife very poor, and did not leave her even food for one meal." Rabbi Aaron said: "I have two thalers hidden in the chest, which I hid away for a certain purpose. Now let her take them." When he woke from his sleep, the Mokhiach went in the morning to the widow, Rabbi Aaron's wife, and told her to search in the chest, and that she would find two thalers there. And she answered: "There is certainly no money in the chest." He urged her, and she searched, and found the two thalers, and also found the hairs wrapped in a rag. And from this you will see how clear and certain their dreams were.

**Summary:** A man from Kitov, an opponent of the Hasidim, tore hairs from the beard of Rabbi Aaron, the Baal Shem Tov's brother-in-law, and Rabbi Gershon put him under a ban and he died. After Rabbi Aaron's death Rabbi Gershon saw him in a dream, understood that it was a kelipah in his form and spat at it, and afterward the real Rabbi Aaron appeared to the Mokhiach of Polonne and told him that thanks to the spitting a mark of sin had been removed from him. He told that in the Garden of Eden he had shown the hairs before the court, and the opponent had been driven out to Gehinnom, and he revealed where he had hidden money for his wife. The widow found the money and the hairs in the chest, exactly as in the dream.

## 80 — The Sick Woman Who Protected the Town

I heard from my friends, who heard it from Rabbi Nachum of Chernobyl: once the Baal Shem Tov was in the town of Chmielnik. There was a proofreader there, and his wife was ill. The Baal Shem Tov's lodging was next to the house of the rabbi, the head of the rabbinical court, the son-in-law of the parnas of the month. The proofreader came to the Baal Shem Tov to ask him for a cure for his wife, and the Baal Shem Tov did not want to. And not only that, but he was angry with him, and said unkind things to him. This seemed hard to the rabbi. In the evening, when everyone had gone, the rabbi asked the Baal Shem Tov: "Why did you not want to heal the woman?" He answered him: "Because she is a righteous woman, and through her weakness she protects the town from the robbers in the forest. When the robbers are gone from the forest, she will return to health of herself." He asked him: "And why were you angry with the proofreader?" He answered: "I saw that judgments rested on him, and with that anger I canceled the judgments from him."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov refused to heal a sick woman in Chmielnik, and was angry with her husband who came to ask. To the town rabbi he explained that the woman was righteous, that her illness protected the town from the robbers in the forest, and that she would recover when the robbers were gone. And he explained his anger at her husband by saying that he had seen judgments on him, and with the anger he had canceled them.

## 81 — "Are You Better Than He Is?"

Once that rabbi saw a man come to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov drew him close and spoke with him. And this man was known as an adulterer. The rabbi asked: "Why did you draw this man close, who is known as an adulterer?" The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "Are you better than he is?" The rabbi examined himself, searched his deeds, and found nothing. He said: "I swear that you are telling a lie!" The Baal Shem Tov said: "I see adultery on you, and tomorrow I will tell you the details." The next day he said to him: "You took upon yourself separation (from your wife), and you broke that undertaking. And I heard tonight that Maimonides ruled that this is as if he had forbidden his wife to himself, and said to her 'You are to me like my mother.' Look in his book; perhaps you will find it there." Then he admitted his words, and in Maimonides' book he found that he had ruled so. And a story like this happened to the Baal Shem Tov a third time. (And see below.) <!-- MATURE: sexual content (an adulterer; a vow of separation from a wife) -->

**Summary:** When a rabbi asked the Baal Shem Tov why he drew close a man known as an adulterer, the Baal Shem Tov asked him whether he was better than he was. The rabbi found no sin in himself, and swore that the Baal Shem Tov was lying. The next day the Baal Shem Tov explained to him that he had taken upon himself to separate from his wife and had broken it, and that according to Maimonides this was like a grave sin, and the rabbi admitted it and found the matter in Maimonides.

## 82 — Rabbi Yosef's Bloodletting

I heard from the rabbi of our community: once the Baal Shem Tov was traveling, and stayed the night at a place, and heard: "Hurry, go back home, for Rabbi Yosef is very weak." He told his servants to turn back, and the servants did not want to, for they said: "Your honor has a creditor at home, and why should we go back?" But they turned back, and he reached home on Friday morning. Before the prayer he went to the mikveh, and after the prayer he called a doctor, and went with him to the sick man. He saw that the sick man was very weak, and had lost the power of speech. He told the doctor to let his blood, and the doctor did not want to, and said that it was very dangerous to let his blood. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "What do you understand? If you do not let his blood, will he live?" The doctor said: "No." "If so, why are you afraid to let his blood?" The Baal Shem Tov ordered his blood to be let at once, and all the people complained about the Baal Shem Tov because of the bloodletting. The Baal Shem Tov went home. About half an hour later the sick man asked them to give him water to drink, and thank God, he recovered.

**Summary:** On the road the Baal Shem Tov heard that Rabbi Yosef was very weak, and went back home although the servants did not want to. The doctor was afraid to let the blood of the sick man, who could no longer speak, and the Baal Shem Tov asked him whether he would live without it, and when the doctor answered no, he ordered the blood let at once. Everyone grumbled at the Baal Shem Tov, but half an hour later the sick man asked for water, and he recovered.

## 83 — Rabbi Yosef the Teacher and the Proclamation

I also heard from him: once, on the way to Chmielnik, the Baal Shem Tov heard a proclamation that Rabbi Yosef the teacher of that town would die. When he reached the town, the people of the town came to him, and wanted to pay him to heal him, and he did not want to heal him, and did not reveal the reason. After the Baal Shem Tov left there, a doctor came and treated him, and he recovered. The doctor was still there, and meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov came back to the town, and they told him that Rabbi Yosef had recovered thanks to the doctor's medicines. While the Baal Shem Tov was there, Rabbi Yosef asked the doctor to prepare him some remedy to drive the lice from his head, which had multiplied greatly because of his long illness. The doctor gave him an ointment to rub on his head. He rubbed his head with this ointment, and died. Then the Baal Shem Tov revealed the reason he had not wanted to heal him: because of the proclamation he had heard.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov heard a proclamation that Rabbi Yosef the teacher would die, and so he refused to heal him, without explaining. A doctor healed Rabbi Yosef, but afterward gave him an ointment against lice, and Rabbi Yosef died from it. Only then did the Baal Shem Tov reveal the proclamation he had heard.

## 84 — The Eulogy for the Informer

I also heard from that rabbi: in the days of the Mokhiach of Polonne there was an informer in a certain town, who caused the people of his town great suffering with his informing. Once he fell ill and died, and the Mokhiach ordered the attendants of the burial society to tell him the time of the funeral. They told him, and he put on his coat, and went with Rabbi Meir Milkes of that town. When all the people saw the Mokhiach going to accompany the dead man, literally the whole town went, men and women. The informer's son was very glad that the Mokhiach was honoring his father, and went himself to accompany him. The Mokhiach walked all the way to the cemetery. When they arrived with the dead man, and laid him down by the shelter, as is the custom, the Mokhiach came near, and stood by the dead man to say a eulogy over him. And the informer's son was very pleased.

Then the Mokhiach began his words with a parable, and said this: "Once a nobleman had a dog, called by the name Britan." As soon as the informer's sons heard the opening of the parable, they understood that he would speak words of disgrace about him. They turned back, to go to the castle, to bring from there the nobleman's servants and also thugs, to shame the Mokhiach or beat him. Rabbi Meir was very frightened, took hold of the Mokhiach by the hem of his garment, and whispered to him that the informer's sons had gone to the castle to inform against him. The Mokhiach did not answer him a word, and moved four cubits back, and did not stop his words.

He went on and said: "This dog was very good to the nobleman, for it always caught all kinds of animals when the nobleman went out hunting, as is the custom of noblemen. One day this dog died" (and the Mokhiach pointed with his hand at the dead man) "and there was great rejoicing among the animals, that they were free of this dog's traps. The fox, the wisest of the animals, said to them: 'Fools, what are you rejoicing about? Had he died before the other dogs learned from him to catch animals, it would have been fitting to rejoice. But now, when the nobleman's other dogs have already learned from him to hunt animals, it would have been better for you if this dog were still alive. For when the other dogs caught animals, the dog Britan came and snatched in his mouth what they had already caught, and brought it to the nobleman, and it was all credited to him. And so all the dogs stopped hunting, for they said: why should we work hard, if it is not credited to us, and the nobleman does not value us for it? But now, when Britan has died, all the dogs will work hard at hunting, to find favor in the nobleman's eyes, so that he will be good to them. And so many hunting dogs have been added against you. And what are you rejoicing about?'" Here ends the parable.

And the meaning: the dog Britan is this informer, who was important in the nobleman's eyes because of his informing against the people of the town. And the other dogs are the other informers, who learned from him his way of informing. When the informers saw that they were not important in the nobleman's eyes, because this informer snatched in his mouth and himself brought all the informing to the lord, they let their hands drop from informing. "But now, when this dog has died, and all the informers know that it will be credited to them, and they will be important in the nobleman's eyes, many informers will rise against you from now on." When he finished his words, all the people understood that he was speaking rightly, and there was great weeping among all the people who stood there.

Afterward the Mokhiach turned the dead man over on his face, struck him with the palms of his hands on his backside, and turned back at once. He went with Rabbi Meir to the wagon that was ready for him by the cemetery, and drove off and fled. On the way Rabbi Meir heard the Mokhiach arguing and answering the informer, and Rabbi Meir did not see the dead man. The Mokhiach showed him that one of the wheels of the wagon was not turning, for the informer was holding it back with his hand, and speaking with him, and thanking him for the disgrace he had done him, and asking him at least from now on not to attack him with such harsh words. And the Baal Shem Tov said that a dog called Britan had stood ready, so that as soon as the informer was buried his soul would be reincarnated in that dog. And the disgrace the Mokhiach did him in this eulogy took him out of that punishment.

And I forgot the main thing: after the Mokhiach finished the meaning of the parable, he said: "This is the meaning of the verse 'So I praised the dead who are already dead': for had he died before the other informers learned from him, it would have been fitting to praise his death. But not so now, when there is no joy, because of 'the living who are still alive,'" that is, because of the other informers who are still left alive.

**Summary:** When an informer who had made the people of his town suffer died, the Mokhiach of Polonne went to eulogize him, and the whole town followed him, and the informer's son was glad. But the Mokhiach told a parable of a nobleman's hunting dog, which while it lived snatched the prey from all the other dogs, and when it died, all the other dogs would begin to hunt, and so he explained to the crowd that now many informers would rise against them, and the crowd wept. The Mokhiach disgraced the dead man and fled, and on the way the dead man held back the wagon wheel to thank him, for according to the Baal Shem Tov this disgrace saved the informer's soul from being reincarnated in a dog.

## 85 — The Informer Compared with the Wicked of the Generation

And I heard from that rabbi that after a time, when, because of our sins, the generation had become unruly, he heard the Mokhiach say: "If only that informer were alive today. He would count with me as one of the circle of the Hasidim, compared with the wicked of the generation at this time."

**Summary:** Years later, when the generation had gone bad, the Mokhiach of Polonne said that he wished that informer were still alive, for compared with the wicked of the new generation he would count as a Hasid.

## 86 — The Dowry and the Promissory Note

I heard from a man who heard it from Rabbi Tzvi the scribe that the Baal Shem Tov made a match with the famous Rabbi Zev Kutzes, between an orphan boy and an orphan girl who were raised in their homes: the orphan girl was raised in the Baal Shem Tov's house, and the orphan boy was raised in Rabbi Zev's house. The Baal Shem Tov undertook to give two hundred zlotys for the dowry. When the wedding came, before the *badeken* (the veiling of the bride), Rabbi Zev told the Baal Shem Tov that he would not go with the groom to the badeken until the Baal Shem Tov paid the two hundred zlotys he had undertaken. This seemed to the Baal Shem Tov like a joke, and he said: "You do not trust me for this sum?" He answered him: "On my faith, I will not change my words. For I have to keep the groom with me to study with him, and 'if there is no flour, there is no Torah.'"

Meanwhile Rabbi Leib Kremener came to the Baal Shem Tov's house. The Baal Shem Tov seated him with honor, and ordered that he be given a bottle of wine to drink. Rabbi Leib said the blessing over it, and the Baal Shem Tov answered amen. Afterward Rabbi Leib began to tell the Baal Shem Tov why he had come: the commissar of the town had sent for him, and had shown him a letter from a nobleman, who lived eight parsahs from Mezhibuzh, who wrote him that the Baal Shem Tov owed him a thousand zlotys from the ransoming of captives, according to the promissory note in his hand, and that he must pay him at once, without fail. And the commissar said that if he did not pay at once, he would take him down in iron chains to that nobleman, and his words would not fall to the ground. And they had sent to tell him to pay at once.

When the Baal Shem Tov heard these words from his mouth, he at once called out loudly to his servant to harness the carriage, and said to Rabbi Leib: "Travel at once to this nobleman, and pay him this sum, and hurry back for the badeken." And he did not let him go home. This seemed astonishing to him, but he did not answer him. He traveled at once to the nobleman. On the way, at an inn, there was an acquaintance of his. They met, and he told him the purpose of the journey, but he hurried very much on his way, and the innkeeper was amazed.

He traveled and reached the nobleman at noon, at the time of their regular meal, and waited until the end of the meal. He came into the nobleman's house, and the nobleman asked him at once whether he had brought him money from the Baal Shem Tov. He answered him: "Yes." The nobleman seated him with honor, and gave him mead to drink. And Rabbi Leib wondered in his heart: how would the payment be made? He thought: "Perhaps the Baal Shem Tov's intention is that one of his good horses will please the nobleman, and the nobleman will accept it instead of the sum." Meanwhile it came into his mind to say to the nobleman: "I need to speak with you about a secret matter." He went with him into a room, and said this to the nobleman: "I know that you are a very righteous and faithful man, and you would not want even a penny's worth of other people's money. And now I want to pay you this sum. But perhaps by the reckoning so much is not owed you for the captives, and you will stumble, Heaven forbid, into robbery." The nobleman answered him with great gratitude: "I love you with a great love for this, that you are saving me from robbery and from punishment in the next world."

At once the nobleman searched for the receipts of the tollkeepers who had been ransomed, and made the reckonings, and took the Baal Shem Tov's promissory note and threw it into the fire, and said to Rabbi Leib: "On the contrary, the Baal Shem Tov is owed two hundred zlotys." He gave him eleven ducats (large gold coins). Rabbi Leib said to him: "Two more zlotys are owed." And the nobleman went on speaking with him with love and affection, because he did not flatter him, and he was dear in his eyes for having saved him from robbery. He gave him the two zlotys, and Rabbi Leib went on his way. When he reached that inn, he jumped down from the wagon to tell these wonders. And the Baal Shem Tov, at home, scolded and said: "That fool is lingering on the road and telling the story!" Afterward Rabbi Leib came to the Baal Shem Tov, and wanted to tell him what had happened, and the Baal Shem Tov answered: "I know. Quick, give me the money, for the day is already late for the badeken." And he was angry with him: why had he stopped on the road on his way back? And afterward he said: "Now it is time for the wedding!" (And he acted like one of the wedding jesters.)

**Summary:** Rabbi Zev Kutzes refused to go to the wedding canopy with the orphan groom until the Baal Shem Tov paid the two hundred zlotys of the dowry. At that very time a demand came from a nobleman to pay at once a thousand zlotys on a promissory note for the ransoming of captives, and the Baal Shem Tov at once sent Rabbi Leib to the nobleman, without money. Rabbi Leib asked the nobleman to check the reckoning so that he would not stumble into robbery, and the nobleman discovered that in fact he owed the Baal Shem Tov exactly two hundred zlotys, burned the note, and paid. The Baal Shem Tov received the money, was angry that Rabbi Leib had lingered on the road to tell the story, and rejoiced at the wedding.

## 87 — The Father of Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz

I heard from the rabbi of our community: once Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz went with the rabbi of Rovno to ransom a captive, for a relative of them both had been taken into prison. Rabbi Pinchas said to the rabbi: "I have heard that the Baal Shem Tov is spending the Sabbath in Kaminka. Let us travel to him; perhaps there will be salvation, and we will not need to drag ourselves along the roads." They agreed, and hurried to find him before he went to the mikveh, for after the mikveh it is impossible to speak a word with him. Even so, they found him when he had already come from the mikveh, and had put on his Sabbath clothes. In the study house his people said to them: "Show yourselves before him, and he will surely know what you need, even without your telling him." They did so, and came to the door of the study house. When he saw them, he waved his hand for them to go.

Afterward Rabbi Pinchas heard that his father, Rabbi Avraham Abba, had come from across the river to the town of Maripalye, and he went to him. For he was afraid that his father would want to spend the Sabbath with the Baal Shem Tov, and in his eyes the Baal Shem Tov counted for nothing, and he always used to say to his son: "Why do you travel to him?" And Rabbi Pinchas was afraid that they would not honor his father in some matter, and his father would surely be insolent before the Baal Shem Tov, and there would be sorrow and shame, Heaven forbid. He asked his father: "Where will you spend the Sabbath?" He said: "What do I have to go to the Baal Shem Tov for? I will spend the Sabbath here, and preach here."

In the morning, when the Baal Shem Tov began "Hodu," the father came to the study house of Kaminka, and stood by the holy ark. The Baal Shem Tov was leading the prayers before the ark, and his father prayed in a loud voice. Rabbi Pinchas was afraid that his father would confuse the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov would rebuke him. But the Baal Shem Tov said nothing to him until after the morning prayer. It was a Sabbath that was Rosh Chodesh, and two Torah scrolls had to be taken out. The Baal Shem Tov took one Torah scroll, and the father took the second Torah scroll, and walked after the Baal Shem Tov to the reading table. The Baal Shem Tov saw that he was holding the Torah scroll, and scolded, and said to someone: "Take the Torah scroll from his hand!" And the father was silent, and did not answer him a word. Rabbi Pinchas wondered at this. After the prayer the father went to the town across the river, to the parnas of the month, and ate there, slept a little, and went to the synagogue to preach.

At the time of the third Sabbath meal the father came to the Baal Shem Tov. The people of Kaminka had a custom that when the Baal Shem Tov was with them, a table was set up for them at the side, and the guests sat with the Baal Shem Tov at the table in the house. When they saw him coming, they called him to sit with them, for they knew him, that he was a *Litvak* (a Lithuanian Jew) and firm in his opinions, and they were afraid he would confuse the Baal Shem Tov or interfere in his words. They called to him: "Rabbi Avraham, come and sit with us," and gave him bottles of mead. When the Baal Shem Tov began to say Torah, the father left his place, and stood facing the Baal Shem Tov in the middle of the house, and looked into his face. Rabbi Pinchas was afraid to tell him not to look into the Baal Shem Tov's face, because of honoring his father, and the father stood facing the Baal Shem Tov, and Rabbi Pinchas was in distress. When the Baal Shem Tov stopped saying Torah, the father went back to his place. Afterward they lit candles and ate meat, and again the Baal Shem Tov said: "We must say one more thing. And I had not intended to say it, except that I heard this thing in the Garden of Eden, in my name." He began to say it, and Rabbi Avraham left his place a second time, and stood facing the Baal Shem Tov until he finished, and went back to his place. They said the Grace after Meals, and prayed the evening prayer.

After the prayer the father said to Rabbi Pinchas: "It is he and no other! It is he and no other!" Rabbi Pinchas said: "What did you see in him?" He said to him: "Do you think I was silent when he rebuked me during the Torah reading? I was waiting for the end. I will tell you, my son: last night I saw a dream, that I went into the Garden of Eden, and the Prince of the Torah came, and said a word of Torah in the name of the Baal Shem Tov. When I woke from my sleep, I remembered it well, and for this thing I came here. And as I heard it there, so I heard it from his holy mouth." And about the matter of the captive I did not hear from that rabbi, and I forgot to ask him.

**Summary:** The father of Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz looked down on the Baal Shem Tov, and Rabbi Pinchas was afraid he would shame him when he came to Kaminka for the Sabbath. The father prayed aloud while the Baal Shem Tov was leading the prayers, and the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and took the Torah scroll from him, and the father was silent. At the third meal the father stood facing the Baal Shem Tov and looked into his face while he said Torah, and in the end he said to his son "It is he and no other," for that night he had dreamed that the Prince of the Torah was saying a word of Torah in the Garden of Eden in the name of the Baal Shem Tov, and he had come to check, and heard from the Baal Shem Tov's mouth exactly the same thing.

## 88 — The Priest in Mezhibuzh

I heard that there was a priest in Mezhibuzh, close to the commissar, and he caused great troubles to the Jews of the town. The people of the town pressed the Baal Shem Tov to pray that he be driven out of the town. He said: "I do not want to provoke him, for he is a great sorcerer, and when I start with him, he will sense it at once." And he did nothing in the matter. Once the priest pressed the people of the town very hard, and they came to the Baal Shem Tov with a great outcry and complaint. He answered them: "What can I do with you? I will start dealing with him, but quietly and gently, so that he will not sense it." He began with prayers, and at once the priest sensed it.

Once the Baal Shem Tov was driving up a hill, and met the priest coming down the hill. The Baal Shem Tov said: "He will have a downfall. And had it been, Heaven forbid, the other way around, it would not have been good for me." And so it was: men came from the lord of the town, took the priest's property and money from him, and removed him from his post, and he had to pay all the Jews in the town, each and every one his claim, until he became poor, and left the town with one horse, in a shabby wagon.

And from Rabbi Leib of Sinami I heard another version: the priest put a spell on the Baal Shem Tov. When the Baal Shem Tov was driving over the bridge by the flour mill, the wagon with the horses was swept away. The Baal Shem Tov held back the wagon, stopped the mill-race, and laid down the planks, and the people got out, and hired gentiles, and brought out the horses with the wagon. Then the Baal Shem Tov said: "Since he knows (that I am working against him), he will surely come to a bad end." And this Rabbi Leib was a great and learned man, and was the brother of Rabbi David the scribe of Anipoli.

**Summary:** A priest in Mezhibuzh caused great suffering to the Jews, and at first the Baal Shem Tov did not want to deal with him, for he was a great sorcerer who would sense it. When the distress grew, he began to pray against him quietly, and the priest sensed it, and according to one version he even tried to harm the Baal Shem Tov with witchcraft on a bridge. In the end the lord of the town took all the priest's property, and he had to pay every Jew his claim, and he left the town poor.

## 89 — The Stolen Wagon

This too I heard from him, and afterward I heard it from the rabbi of our community: once a shopkeeper from the town of Sde Lavan came from Breslau, with a big covered wagon full of cloth goods. He arrived at night, and did not want to open the shop at night. He ordered the horses unharnessed from the wagon, and said in his heart: "Who could steal such a big wagon?" And he left the wagon with the goods outside. Afterward a thief came, harnessed other horses to the wagon, and fled with the wagon and all the goods. In the morning they saw that the wagon with the goods had been stolen, and began to run and search in all the streets and at all the crossroads, and did not find it, and did not catch the thief. The merchant sent a letter to the Baal Shem Tov. When the messenger came to the Baal Shem Tov, he found him standing by the mezuzah, ready to travel to the town of Drazna for a circumcision, for they had honored him with performing the circumcision. He took the letter from the messenger, and said to him: "Wait until I come back."

The messenger waited. The Baal Shem Tov stayed the night on the way, and in the morning drove to the town. As he came near the town, the Baal Shem Tov saw from afar that the wagon with the goods was also traveling to the town, by another road. He said to Rabbi Tzvi the scribe: "Look, look at the wagon traveling with two horses." He said: "I see it." The Baal Shem Tov said: "The messenger they sent to me came for this wagon, with the goods in it, which were stolen. When we come to the town, ask where the traveler is lodging, and tell him to give you the wagon with the goods, for it is stolen goods in his hands."

When they reached the town, Rabbi Tzvi the scribe went at once to the man's lodging, and saw him standing and praying, and did not dare to tell him the words, for the man prayed like a simple, upright person. The scribe went back to the Baal Shem Tov and told him this. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Go at once, for he will leave at once with the goods, and the wagon with the goods will be lost." The scribe went a second time, and found him eating the morning meal. He asked the innkeeper whether the man had drunk a lot of vodka, as thieves do, and the innkeeper said that he had drunk only two groschen's worth, like anybody. And again the scribe left him empty-handed. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Go and tell him all the signs: that he stood in the forest three days, until all the riders who were searching for him had passed, and afterward stayed the night at such-and-such a place. And tell him all the places where he stayed the night, up to the town of Drazna." The scribe went, and told him all the signs. Then the thief's hands went weak, and he said: "It is true, that is how it is. Take the wagon with the goods." And the scribe ordered the innkeeper to guard the wagon with the goods.

When they came to eat at the circumcision feast, the thief said: "Now I am poor like the rest of the poor. I will go and eat at the circumcision feast." As they ate and drank mead, the thief stood by the Baal Shem Tov and said: "I have a great question for your honor. If your honor knows and sees how thieves steal, and their place and the places where they stay the night, surely you can see better things than these. Why should you look at low things? Better to look at good things." And the Baal Shem Tov said Torah on this question until the afternoon prayer, and did not finish this Torah.

**Summary:** A thief took a wagon full of goods from a shopkeeper in Sde Lavan, and the shopkeeper sent a letter to the Baal Shem Tov, who was on his way to a circumcision. On the way the Baal Shem Tov saw the stolen wagon traveling to the same town, and sent Rabbi Tzvi the scribe, who at first did not dare to suspect a man who prayed with such simplicity, until the Baal Shem Tov gave him all the signs of the thief's route, and the thief admitted it and gave back the goods. At the circumcision feast the thief asked the Baal Shem Tov why he looked at low things like thefts and not at good things, and the Baal Shem Tov said Torah on this question until the afternoon prayer.

## 90 — The Baal Shem Tov's Funeral

I heard from the rabbi of our community: at the Baal Shem Tov's funeral Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka was greatly amazed: "What is this, that I see nothing?" And when he came back from the cemetery, he saw great and awesome things, and said to Rabbi Zev Kutzes: "Surely that is how it must be."

**Summary:** At the Baal Shem Tov's funeral Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka was amazed that he saw nothing special. Only when he came back from the cemetery did he see great and awesome things, and he said that surely that is how it must be.

## 91 — Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka Travels to the Land of Israel

I heard from the rabbi of our community: when Rabbi Nachman wanted to travel to the Holy Land, on a matter of ransoming captives, he went to the Baal Shem Tov's grave with Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka. When he came back from the cemetery, he rejoiced greatly and said: "The Baal Shem Tov commanded me to travel to the Holy Land." Rabbi Yosef was greatly amazed and said: "And where did he speak with you?" Rabbi Nachman was amazed at him and said: "And did you not see that he spoke with me, and stood beside me?"

On his journey Rabbi Nachman spent the Sabbath with the rabbi the Mokhiach, and I was there too. I, the author, heard that there were sharp words between Rabbi Nachman and the Mokhiach: Rabbi Nachman spoke sharp words to the Mokhiach and laughed, and the Mokhiach took offense at Rabbi Nachman, and said: "A person cannot be saved from a stray thought. Even if he prays without a stray thought, near the end of the prayer the thought falls on him that he will soon finish his prayer, and that is a stray thought, and a very subtle one. And from every transgression one accuser is created. And because it is a very subtle thought, this thought clothes itself in a Torah scholar and torments him."

All the people of the town accompanied Rabbi Nachman, and I was among them, and also the rabbi and the Mokhiach. The Mokhiach wanted to travel with him as far as the town of Ladyzhin, but because the Mokhiach took offense, "how is it proper manners to leave him," he did not go. They prayed the afternoon prayer in the field. The rabbi put on tefillin and led the prayers before the ark, and Rabbi Nachman prayed without tallit and tefillin. I did not see the Mokhiach, whether he prayed there, and it seems to me that he went into the town to pray.

A year later Rabbi Nachman passed away, in the month of Tammuz. And in that month Rabbi Nachman was also in Nemirov, and Rabbi Mendele of Premishlan joined him and received from him, and was greatly amazed at the wisdom of the Baal Shem Tov. And I heard that Rabbi Nachman passed away on the Sabbath. Rabbi Mendele sat with him at night, and Rabbi Nachman made Kiddush over the wine, and drank the proper amount, and sat in his Sabbath clothes. Rabbi Mendele went to eat the Sabbath meal, and while they were eating the last course, Rabbi Nachman sent for him. Rabbi Mendele found him dying, and he was studying the Zohar as his soul departed. He died at the time of the afternoon prayer, and went to his rest.

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka went to the Baal Shem Tov's grave, and came back joyful, for the Baal Shem Tov had stood beside him and told him to travel to the Land of Israel, but Rabbi Yosef, who was with him, saw nothing. On the way he had sharp words with the Mokhiach, who said that even a subtle thought in prayer creates an accuser, and the whole town accompanied him. Rabbi Nachman passed away a year later on the Sabbath, studying the Zohar as his soul departed.

## 92 — Rabbi Mendele and Leading the Prayers Before the Ark

I heard that the rabbi (of Nemirov) praised Rabbi Mendele's prayer very highly. Rabbi Mendele came from the town of Tsikanovka to take leave of the rabbi before his journey (to the Land of Israel), and spent the Sabbath there, and stayed there about five days. He came on Wednesday, while the rabbi was praying the afternoon prayer, and Rabbi Mendele's wagon with the horses stood there, and they did not unload anything from the wagon. I was a teacher then, and the way was long, and I was late in coming. When I came, I asked his people: "Why are they not unloading the wagon?" They answered me that he did not want to stay with the rabbi, for the rabbi needs to lead the prayers before the ark, and he too wants to lead the prayers before the ark. So he wants them to give him lodgings near the rabbi, so that he can meet with the rabbi. And they did not let me pray, for Rabbi Mendele was still smoking his pipe. And in the house where they pray too, they did not let anyone pray, for the rabbi had not yet finished his prayer. And whoever came late, they did not let him pray, so that Rabbi Mendele would have a minyan.

When the rabbi finished his prayer, he went out of the house with his minyan, and Rabbi Mendele came in with us to lead the prayers before the ark. He stood in another place, as the law requires, and prayed the afternoon prayer. After he finished his prayer, he sat near his place, and wrapped up his tefillin. The rabbi came in, and greeted him. The rabbi asked him: "Why are they not unloading the wagon?" And Rabbi Mendele stammered in his answer, for for twelve years he had not spoken, except in writing. And when he spoke, he would catch hold of his speech, and weigh it before letting it out of his mouth. And especially since it was not good manners to give such an answer. He stammered, and no answer at all was heard from him, until I could not bear it. Although I was very ashamed before the rabbi, I had to say: "It is because of the prayer: your honor needs to lead the afternoon prayer and the Sabbath prayers before the ark, and he too needs to lead the prayers." The rabbi answered: "For that there is no obstacle at all. 'In a place where there are no men, strive to be a man.'"

Afterward the rabbi ordered the things brought in from the wagon, and gave Rabbi Mendele his house of seclusion, and the rabbi himself sat in the sukkah, although it was still cold weather. Rabbi Mendele came on Wednesday, and left on Monday. The rabbi sat in the cold sukkah, and gave him the small house where they pray, and Rabbi Mendele slept there. And the rabbi never slept there.

When Rabbi Mendele left, Rabbi David of Linitz came, for he was thinking of traveling to the Holy Land, and first wanted to meet Rabbi Mendele. He did not find him in Nemirov, and stayed the night with the rabbi. And I heard that the rabbi praised Rabbi Mendele's prayer very highly, and said this: "I too had become like a simple man, and had forgotten the way of prayer, and they reminded me of the way of prayer." He spoke in the plural, of him and of Rabbi Fridel, for Rabbi Fridel had led the prayers before the ark at the rabbi's before Rabbi Mendele came, on Monday morning. And the rabbi said this: "When they say 'Blessed be He and blessed be His name,' did you see Rabbi Fridel of Brody, a householder, how his prayer goes?"

And I also heard that once the rabbi let Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz lead the Sabbath prayers before the ark, in Polonne. And the rabbi of our community, when I told him the story, was greatly amazed, and said: "I heard from him (from Rabbi Pinchas) that he used to say: 'I will always be sorry that I gave up the prayer to them.'" And about Rabbi Mendele: he was then thirty-six years old, and the rabbi was close to sixty, and perhaps more. And see how the early tzaddikim behaved with such great humility.

**Summary:** Rabbi Mendele, who for twelve years had spoken only in writing, came to take leave of the rabbi of Nemirov before his journey to the Land of Israel, and did not want to unload his wagon, for both of them wanted to lead the prayers before the ark. The rabbi gave way to him, gave him his house, and himself sat in a cold sukkah for five days. The rabbi, who was nearly sixty, praised the prayer of the thirty-six-year-old Rabbi Mendele, and said that he and Rabbi Fridel had reminded him what prayer is, and from this one sees the humility of the early tzaddikim.

## 93 — Honor as a Heavy Burden

I heard that Rabbi Mendele complained before the rabbi about the honor people showed him, for I heard that in the town of Old Konstantin the whole town came out to meet him. He said to the rabbi: "Honor is like a heavy burden on me." The rabbi answered him that the Baal Shem Tov always used to pray that people would speak ill of him. And Rabbi Mendele said that he too had thought of such a remedy.

**Summary:** Rabbi Mendele complained that the honor shown him was like a heavy burden. The rabbi answered that the Baal Shem Tov used to pray that people would speak ill of him, and Rabbi Mendele said that he too had thought of such a remedy.

## 94 — Rabbi Nachman's Cold Mikveh

Once I heard from Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka, who said: "When I was a great Hasid, I went every day to a cold mikveh, such as no one in this generation could bear. And when I came home, I found a very hot mikveh, so hot that the walls almost burned, and I did not feel the heat for about an hour. And even so I could not rid myself of stray thoughts, until I had to make use of the wisdom of the Baal Shem Tov."

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka told that he immersed every day in a very cold mikveh, and for a whole hour did not feel the heat of a very hot mikveh. And even so he could not rid himself of stray thoughts, until he made use of the wisdom of the Baal Shem Tov.

## 95 — The Thought During Prayer

And I heard from Rabbi Mendel of Bar, who said: "In the days of my youth, in a moment of fervor during prayer, the thought rose in my mind: 'How do I dare to pray before God, when I am full of sins?' And my heart broke within me. So it went for a long time, and I could not rid myself of this thought, for it seemed to me that it was a good thought. Afterward I thought in my heart: 'Why does this thought not fall into my mind and my heart when I sit down to eat at the bowl?' And then I pushed this thought away."

**Summary:** Rabbi Mendel of Bar told that in his youth, during prayer, the thought would fall on him that he was full of sins and not worthy to pray, and it seemed to him that it was a good thought. But when he noticed that this thought never came to him while he was eating, he understood that it was not good, and pushed it away.

## 96 — The Mokhiach in Lvov, and the Madman Who Pretended to Be Dead

I heard in the rabbi's house in Nemirov, from the mouth of Rabbi Moshe, who heard it from the Mokhiach: the Mokhiach dreamed that they taught him a sermon, and he remembered some things from it, and told the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Tell me some of the things you remembered." And the Baal Shem Tov told him to preach first at home from the book, and afterward he would know how to preach well. "And the Baal Shem Tov commanded me to travel to the city of Lvov, as they had commanded me in the dream. And the Baal Shem Tov commanded me to preach precisely in the great synagogue, and to stay in the house of the parnas of the month. I set out, and preached in all the places.

"Once I preached in a certain town, and my Torah flowed like a gushing spring. There was a rich man there from Lvov, who heard the sermon, and it pleased him very much. And that sermon I forgot, and traveled on to another town. In the street the boys mocked me, and called after me: 'Preacher, preacher!' Meanwhile that rich man came, rebuked them and said: 'I heard that he is a great preacher. Leave him alone!'

"When I came to Lvov, to the house of the parnas of the month, the parnas was not at home, and I wanted to stay there, and the servant of the house would not let me. He said: 'Here the custom is to stay at the community house, where all the preachers stay.'" And the Mokhiach insisted, and wanted to stay precisely with the parnas. Meanwhile the parnas came, and saw that the Mokhiach was insisting on staying precisely in his house. He said: "Here it is not the custom to stay with the parnas of the month. But since you insist, I will give you a place." And he gave him a small room among his rooms, and the Mokhiach put his things there and sat there. The day was still long, and the Mokhiach took a book of Psalms and said Psalms, and the master of the house went about his business in his house. When he heard him saying the Psalms, it pleased him very much, and the Mokhiach found favor in his eyes, and he went in to him.

The parnas asked him in which synagogue he wanted to preach, and he said: "In the great synagogue." He answered him: "Here it is not the custom to preach like that. First one preaches in the kloyz (a small study house), afterward in the great study house, and when the preacher finds favor in the eyes of the people of the city, then he preaches in the great synagogue." And the Mokhiach said: "No! I want to preach first precisely in the great synagogue." The parnas said: "First one must get permission from the rabbi of our community." Meanwhile the rich man who had heard him in another town came, and told the parnas that he was a great preacher. The parnas said: "I will go to the rabbi, so that he will let you preach in the synagogue."

The Mokhiach had no fine clothes, only one coat, a kaftan. The parnas asked him: "In what clothes do you want to go to the synagogue to preach?" He said: "I have only this garment, this kaftan. If you want to lend me clothes, I will wear them." At once the parnas took out of his chest his fur coat and his coat, of wool. The Mokhiach said: "I do not wear woolen clothes." And the parnas brought out silk clothes for him, and he put them on. The parnas went to the rabbi, and asked him for permission for the Mokhiach. The rabbi said: "Let him preach, and I too will go to hear the sermon." And the rabbi asked the shammes to let him know when the Mokhiach went to preach. As soon as the Mokhiach went to preach, the shammes told the rabbi, and the rabbi ran to the synagogue, and the sermon pleased everyone very much.

After the evening prayer the rabbi asked the Mokhiach to be his guest. The Mokhiach said: "I am not my own master." The rabbi went to the parnas, and asked him that the Mokhiach be his guest, and the parnas did not dare to refuse the rabbi, and gave him the guest. The rabbi asked him: "Are you a disciple of the Baal Shem Tov, and is your name Rabbi Leib?" He said: "Yes." The rabbi asked him about the Baal Shem Tov's appearance, and the Mokhiach described his appearance in words, and the rabbi said: "Yes." The Mokhiach asked the rabbi: "How do you know his appearance?" The rabbi said: "I lay down to sleep a little before the afternoon prayer, and I saw the Baal Shem Tov, and he said: 'Let my disciple preach in the great synagogue.'" And the rabbi also said that his uncle had been the greatest man of the generation (and it seems to me that his name was Rabbi Avraham, but I have forgotten his name): "I was with him at the hour of his death, and I heard him command his son and say: 'A man will arise, and his name is Israel, son of the midwife. Know that he is true, and his Torah is true, and hold fast to him.'" The rabbi kept the Mokhiach with him three weeks, and asked him to reveal to him everything he had received from the Baal Shem Tov. The rabbi wanted to travel to the Baal Shem Tov, and died a few days later.

They gave him thirty zlotys there, and if he had wanted to take more, they would have given him two hundred zlotys. And in Lvov they made him white Sabbath clothes. On the way back he came to the Baal Shem Tov, and spent the Sabbath with him. On Friday night, when they came from the prayer, the Baal Shem Tov stood by the table and said "Shalom Aleichem," and the Mokhiach said "Shalom Aleichem" while walking back and forth in the house. The Mokhiach saw that the Baal Shem Tov turned back, toward the door, and waved his hand. And it came into his mind that the Baal Shem Tov was making a show before him as if he saw something, for the Mokhiach had fallen into pride. The Baal Shem Tov turned like that two or three times, and the Mokhiach was afraid to ask him.

At the end of the Sabbath, after Havdalah, the Baal Shem Tov's custom was to lie down and rest, and smoke a pipe, and tell what he had seen on the Sabbath. He told on and on, and the Mokhiach asked: "What was the thing I saw, that your honor turned around two or three times while saying 'Shalom Aleichem'?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "The Satan wanted to snatch you out of my house, and I rebuked him, and prayed for you while saying 'Shalom Aleichem.' And when he came the second time, I said to him: 'He will have a punishment, but you will not take him out of my hand.'" And so it was: when he went home, he and his household fell ill, and the Baal Shem Tov had great trouble until he and his household recovered. And the Mokhiach forgot all the sermons, and the Mokhiach said that many adventures and much labor passed over him before he could preach again. The main part of the story I heard from Rabbi Moshe, the husband of the sister of the Rabbi of Polonne, who heard it from the Mokhiach himself, in Nemirov. And from Rabbi Yosef son of Rabbi Moshe of Polonne I also heard several versions of this story.

But once Rabbi David, the preacher of Tulchin, the grandson of the tzaddik Rabbi David Purkes, spent the Sabbath with the rabbi of our community, and told me a story: a man brought his mad son to the Baal Shem Tov. When he was one parsah from the town, the madman steeled himself and made himself like a dead man. His father brought him to the town to bury him, and laid him in the women's synagogue. The Baal Shem Tov said to the scribe: "Today we must beat a dead man." And when they brought the dead man, he said to the scribe: "Take a thick shoe strap, and go to the synagogue, and drive the burial society out of there." And he ordered him to be beaten. The scribe beat him, and he did not get up and did not move. The Baal Shem Tov ordered him to be put in a house, the house to be shut, and him to be guarded until the festival of Passover. Until Passover there were three weeks. On the night of the Seder he ordered him to be seated at the table, and propped up with pillows so that he could sit, and a man to be placed beside him to put into his mouth everything they were doing, both food and drink, and to guard him so that he would not run away, "for if he runs away, it will be hard to find him." At the third cup he could not bear it, and jumped up, and leaped out of the house, and ran away. They searched for him a whole day and night, until they found him, and the Baal Shem Tov healed him.

**Summary:** The Mokhiach of Polonne dreamed a sermon, and the Baal Shem Tov sent him to preach in the great synagogue of Lvov. Although it was not the custom to let a new preacher preach there, the rabbi of Lvov saw the Baal Shem Tov in a dream asking that his disciple be allowed to preach, and it turned out that the rabbi's uncle had commanded before his death to hold fast to a man who would arise named Israel, son of the midwife.
