When the Mokhiach came back and grew proud, the Baal Shem Tov saw the Satan wanting to snatch him away, and saved him, but the Mokhiach and his household fell ill, and he forgot all his sermons. At the end is told of a madman who pretended to be dead, and the Baal Shem Tov ordered him beaten and guarded until the night of the Seder, and at the third cup he ran away, and after they found him the Baal Shem Tov healed him.

## 97 — The Martyrs of Pavoloch

The story of the false accusation in Pavoloch: the rabbi of Korostyshev, who was called Rabbi David, fled, and wanted to flee all the way to the land of Wallachia, for all the important men in the region were afraid that they too would be falsely accused. When he reached Mezhibuzh, the Baal Shem Tov held him back. Several times the Baal Shem Tov said that the accused would be saved. Afterward, when they were killed, a letter came to Rabbi David about the harsh and bitter tortures that were done to each and every one of them. Rabbi David came with the letter to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov was very grieved.

That day was the Sabbath eve. The Baal Shem Tov went to the mikveh, and wept greatly, and prayed the afternoon prayer with great bitterness, until the disciples close to him could not raise their heads. They said: "Perhaps when he begins to welcome the Sabbath, he will welcome it with joy." But he prayed the welcoming of the Sabbath and the evening prayer also in bitterness of heart, and made Kiddush over the wine weeping, and washed his hands, and sat at the table, and went into the room to sleep there. He threw himself down on the ground, and lay there a very long time. His household and the guests waited, until the candles began to go out. His wife went and said to him: "The candles will soon go out, and the guests are waiting." He said to her: "Let them eat, and say the Grace after Meals, and go." And his wife too went into the room where the Baal Shem Tov was, and lay down on her bed.

The Baal Shem Tov still lay on the ground, his hands and feet stretched out. Rabbi David stood by the door to see what the end would be, and grew tired of standing, and took a bench and sat by the door. At about midnight he heard the Baal Shem Tov say to his wife: "Cover your face." In one moment the room lit up, and the light could be seen through the cracks. And the Baal Shem Tov said: "Welcome, Rabbi Akiva!" And to the other holy martyrs too, whose names Rabbi David did not know, he said: "Welcome." And Rabbi David recognized Rabbi Akiva by his voice.

The Baal Shem Tov said to the holy martyrs: "I decree that you go and take revenge on the enemy, the accuser, who is called Strigator." The holy martyrs answered and said: "Let this word not come out of his mouth again, and let what you have spoken be canceled, for your honor does not know his own power. When your honor disturbed the Sabbath, there was a great uproar in the Garden of Eden, and everyone fled from all the palaces, as before a sword. And when we came to a higher palace, there too they fled, and did not know from what, until we came to a high place, where they know. There they cried out to us: 'Hurry and go and quiet the tears of the Baal Shem Tov!' And we will tell your honor: all the sufferings a person has in his life are not like garlic peels compared with the sufferings we bore for the sanctification of His name, may He be blessed and exalted. And the evil inclination does its part, and confused our thoughts by a hair's breadth, although we pushed it away with both hands. Even so, we had to bear half an hour of Gehinnom. Afterward we went into the Garden of Eden, and began to claim that we wanted to take revenge on our enemies, and they answered us: 'His hour (the enemy's) still stands. And if you want to take revenge, you must take upon yourselves another life in the world.'

"And we said: thank God that we gave ourselves for the sanctification of God's name, and had to bear the pain of Gehinnom, for all sufferings are like garlic peels compared with that half hour of Gehinnom. And what will happen if we are born again into this world, and perhaps we will go bad, Heaven forbid, and have no repair? So it is better for us not to take revenge and not to come back into the world. And so we ask your honor to cancel what he said."

The Baal Shem Tov asked them: "Why did Heaven mislead me, and not reveal to me that you were destined to be holy martyrs (to be killed for the sanctification of God's name)?" They answered him: "If you had known of it, you would have prayed much more, and canceled the decree, and from it, Heaven forbid, a great trouble would have come. So they did not let your honor know." And from the rabbi of our community I heard that the Baal Shem Tov said that Heaven had promised him to cancel the decree, but a certain preacher preached in Brody, and made himself a living from the captives, and stirred up quarrels, and caused the captives to be killed, because of our many sins.

**Summary:** After a blood libel in Pavoloch the Baal Shem Tov promised several times that the accused would be saved, but they were tortured and killed, and the Baal Shem Tov prayed on the Sabbath eve with weeping and bitterness, and lay on the ground all night. At midnight Rabbi Akiva and the other holy martyrs came to him, and when he decreed that they take revenge on the accuser, they asked him to cancel the decree, for revenge would require them to be born again into the world, and they preferred not to take the risk. They explained that Heaven had not revealed to the Baal Shem Tov that they would be killed, because then he would have canceled the decree by prayer, and according to another version, the decree was not canceled because of a preacher from Brody who stirred up quarrels.

## 98 — The Two Candles

I heard from Rabbi Aaron of our community, who heard it from the great rabbi of Siptifke: in their area there was an agunah who held a lease (an *arenda*). The Baal Shem Tov saw that once she sinned with her gentile servant. She had two brothers, in two villages, and the matter became known to the brothers, and it was a shame to them, and they thought that she would leave the fold (convert), Heaven forbid. They made a plan: each of them would ask her to come to his village, and toward evening they would both go and put her to death. That night the Baal Shem Tov sat, and two candles burned before him. Suddenly one candle went out, and he lit it from the second candle. And at once the second candle went out, and he lit it as before. And he heard a voice saying to him: "Murderer, how do you come to two candles!" And he understood the matter. He took a horse, and rode it at a gallop, and arrived there before they had put her to death, and saved her from their hands. She became a complete penitent, and afterward they found her standing in the mikveh at midnight. <!-- MATURE: adultery; two brothers plan to kill their sister -->

**Summary:** An agunah sinned with her servant, and her two brothers were ashamed and planned to kill her. That night the two candles before the Baal Shem Tov went out again and again, and a voice said to him "Murderer, how do you come to two candles," and he understood, rode fast, and saved her. She repented completely, and they found her standing in the mikveh at midnight.

## 99 — The Merchant Whose Son Traveled to Breslau, and the Robbers in the Forest

There was a shopkeeper in Mezhibuzh, called the "gevant shnayder" (the cloth cutter), whose son traveled to Breslau and stayed there a very long time. His mother was very worried, and told her husband to go to the Baal Shem Tov to ask him where he was. The Baal Shem Tov was not important in her husband's eyes, and he did not want to go, and his wife pestered him until he had to go. He came to the Baal Shem Tov, and laughed, and said: "My wife said that you know certain things, so I came for you to tell me where my son is." He answered him: "Things like that I look at in the synagogue." He took his pipe, and went to the synagogue. He came back and said: "I saw him. He is still in Breslau, and walking about in a German's hat." (That was the custom there: when a deal is closed, they exchange hats, and that is the sign that the deal is closed.) The man made himself a note, to remember on what day the deal had been made.

When his son came back from his journey, his father asked him on what day the deal had been closed, and he told him: "On such a day," just as the father had noted, "and we exchanged hats." And he asked him: "Why did you not send me a letter by the post, as every time?" He said to him: "Because on the way a good buyer came my way, and I sold him the goods, and I had to bring the goods to the merchant's house. I turned off the road the post goes by, and bought other goods there, and went to Breslau, and because of that I was late coming home."

Once the shopkeeper wanted to travel to the town of Sde Lavan with goods, and was afraid, for they said there were robbers on that road. His wife said to him: "I heard that the Baal Shem Tov is traveling. Give his servant some gift, so that he will let you know on what day the Baal Shem Tov is traveling, and travel together with him, and you will have no fear." And he traveled together with the Baal Shem Tov. When they came to the forest where the robbers were, the Baal Shem Tov ordered his servant to stop, to let the horses graze. The man was very frightened. The Baal Shem Tov ordered the wagons placed facing each other. The Baal Shem Tov sat in the middle, opened the book of the Zohar, and looked into it. The robbers came out of the forest, and when they came near, trembling seized them, and they fled. When they were far away, the robbers said: "Let us go at them once more." And when they came near, trembling seized them again. When they had gone far off, the robbers did not want to go back at them, and said: "This is no simple thing." When the merchant saw this, he fell on the Baal Shem Tov's neck and kissed him, and said: "Now I know what your honor's greatness is!" The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "You think you know me? You do not know me yet." And this story I do not remember from whom I heard, and it seems to me that I heard it from the rabbi of our community.

**Summary:** A shopkeeper from Mezhibuzh, who looked down on the Baal Shem Tov, asked him under pressure from his wife about his son who was delayed in Breslau, and the Baal Shem Tov saw in the synagogue that the son was still there and had just closed a deal, and when the son came back, it turned out to be exactly so. Another time the shopkeeper traveled with the Baal Shem Tov through a robbers' forest, and the Baal Shem Tov sat between the wagons and studied the Zohar, and the robbers trembled twice as they came near, and fled. When the shopkeeper kissed him and said that now he knew his greatness, the Baal Shem Tov answered that he did not know him yet.

## 100 — The Unfit Animal

Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to Kaminka, and near the town he heard a proclamation to remove the town's shochet from his post. He said: "I will remove him, and arrange some livelihood for him." For he was very careful not to harm any person's livelihood. When he came to Rabbi Baruch's house, Rabbi Baruch made preparations, and slaughtered several animals, and one animal was found to be unfit, and it was hung in the passageway. The Baal Shem Tov was smoking his pipe in the passageway, and Rabbi Baruch came into the passageway. The Baal Shem Tov told him to cut a piece of meat from this animal and roast it for him. Rabbi Baruch thought he was saying it as a joke, and said: "Your honor will not eat from this animal." He said: "I will certainly eat from it." When Rabbi Baruch saw that he was saying it in earnest, he said: "This animal is unfit." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "But this animal is asking me to eat from it. Tell me, why did it become unfit?"

He sent for the shochet, and asked him why this animal was unfit, and the shochet said to him: "There are opinions among the codifiers: some declare it fit and some declare it unfit." And the Baal Shem Tov ordered a piece of meat cut and roasted. The Baal Shem Tov saw that the thing seemed hard to his people, and said that they should send to Polonne, to Rabbi Shmuel, the authority on Jewish law, and bring a letter from him before the meat was roasted. At once they sent a messenger, riding a good horse, and he came with a letter in his hand from the authority on Jewish law that the animal was kosher. And the Baal Shem Tov removed the shochet from his post, and did not want to arrange a livelihood for him.

**Summary:** On the way to Kaminka the Baal Shem Tov heard a proclamation to remove the town's shochet, and planned to arrange another livelihood for him. At Rabbi Baruch's he asked to eat from an animal the shochet had declared unfit, for "the animal is asking to be eaten," and the shochet admitted that there are opinions that declare it fit. An authority on Jewish law from Polonne ruled that the animal was kosher, and the Baal Shem Tov removed the shochet, and no longer wanted to arrange a livelihood for him.

## 101 — Rabbi Nachman's "This Too Is for the Good"

Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka had a trait: about everything he saw, or that happened to him, he would say "Good, and for the good." And his faith was as strong as an iron pillar. Once soldiers were quartered with the Jews in Mezhibuzh. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Pray that the soldiers will not be quartered with the Jews." Rabbi Nachman said to him: "It is for the good." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "How fine it is that you were not in Haman's generation, for you would have said of the decree too that it was good, and you would have no virtue except that Haman was hanged, which is also good."

Once Rabbi Nachman traveled with the Rabbi of Polonne from Nemirov to the Baal Shem Tov for the Sabbath. They traveled on Friday, and met a nobleman in a big carriage, who was also traveling on that road, and was driving slowly. They were afraid to pass him. The rabbi clapped his hands together and said: "Oh, oh, we will desecrate the Sabbath, Heaven forbid!" And he was in great distress. And Rabbi Nachman said: "It is for the good." And that added to the rabbi's distress. A short time later they met a great caravan of wagons with loads. The snow was very deep, and they could not possibly pass them, for they too were traveling on this road, in the same direction. When the gentiles with the loads saw the nobleman, they all turned off the road, and they passed the caravan. Afterward they came to a crossroads, and the nobleman went his way, and they arrived while it was still broad day. All this I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne.

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka said about everything "Good, and for the good," even when the Baal Shem Tov asked him to pray that the soldiers would not be quartered with the Jews. The Baal Shem Tov told him that if he had lived in Haman's generation, he would have said that the decree too was good. Once, on a Friday, he and the Rabbi of Polonne were stuck behind a nobleman's carriage that was driving slowly, and the rabbi was afraid they would desecrate the Sabbath, and Rabbi Nachman said it was for the good. And indeed, when they reached a great caravan that could not be passed in the snow, everyone cleared the road for the nobleman, and they arrived in time.

## 102 — Praying in a House Full of Gentiles

And from the rabbi of our community I heard: once Rabbi Nachman was traveling with the Baal Shem Tov, and they came to a village at the time of the afternoon prayer. The Baal Shem Tov said to the attendant: "Go to the house, and see whether it is possible to pray there." And Rabbi Nachman took his tallit and tefillin bag, and went to the house. The attendant said to the Baal Shem Tov: "The house is full of gentiles." The Baal Shem Tov said: "Go and call Rabbi Nachman to sit in the wagon, for the time of the afternoon prayer is getting late." The attendant went in, and found him wrapped in tallit and tefillin and praying. The attendant told him to come out, and he did not hear. The attendant came out and told the Baal Shem Tov that Rabbi Nachman was wrapped in tallit and tefillin and praying. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Surely he will cause the gentiles some trouble." Meanwhile the castle caught fire, and people came and drove out all the gentiles to save the castle. The house was left empty, and then the Baal Shem Tov too went to pray.

**Summary:** In a village Rabbi Nachman went in to pray the afternoon prayer in a house full of gentiles, and did not hear when they called him to come out. The Baal Shem Tov said that surely Rabbi Nachman would cause the gentiles some trouble, and at once the castle caught fire, and all the gentiles went out to save it. The house emptied, and the Baal Shem Tov too went in to pray.

## 103 — The Rabbi of Polonne's Quickness with Tefillin

I saw that the Rabbi of Polonne would touch his tefillin before the Amidah. And after the Amidah, when he finished the blessing "who blesses His people Israel with peace," he touched his tefillin at once. And I wrote this so as to learn from him quickness in the service of the Creator. And during the cantor's repetition he always looked in the prayer book, and listened to the cantor.

**Summary:** The Rabbi of Polonne touched his tefillin right before the Amidah and right after it, and during the cantor's repetition he looked in the prayer book and listened to the cantor. The author recorded this so as to learn from him quickness in the service of God.

## 104 — The Prayer on the Way to the Lord

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne that once he traveled to the lord of Rashkov about a matter he needed, and prayed on the way. When he reached the prayer "And He, being merciful" of Monday or Thursday, he clothed his request as though the Shechinah were praying a similar request before the God of Israel. After the prayer he went back home, and said, out of his great humility: "I would not tell this, except that I know that now I am not at that level."

**Summary:** When the Rabbi of Polonne traveled to the lord about a matter he needed, he prayed on the way, and in "And He, being merciful" he clothed his request as if the Shechinah herself were making such a request. Out of humility he said that he told this only because he was no longer at that level.

## 105 — Going Up in a Storm

I heard from the rabbi of our community, who heard it from Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka, that the Baal Shem Tov hoped to go up to Heaven in a storm, like Elijah the Prophet. And he grieved over it with a great grief. His household thought he was grieving over the passing of his wife, and they asked him, for they knew that this was not his way. He answered: "I am grieving over these holy states of mind, which must rest in the earth. For I hoped to go up in a storm, and now that I am 'half a body' (for my wife has died), it is impossible. And for this I grieve."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov hoped to go up to Heaven in a storm like Elijah the Prophet, without dying. After his wife died he grieved greatly, and his household thought it was over her passing, but he explained that now that he was "half a body," it was no longer possible for him to go up in a storm, and his holy states of mind would have to rest in the earth.

## 106 — Sixty Years

I also heard that the Baal Shem Tov said: "If the redeemer does not come within sixty years, I must come back to this world."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov said that if the Messiah did not come within sixty years, he would have to come back to this world.

## 107 — The Words Before Shavuot

And the rabbi of our community said that he heard from his grandfather, who spent the Sabbath with the Baal Shem Tov on the last Sabbath before Shavuot, the festival on which he passed away. The Baal Shem Tov said something, and he had a scribe. And the rabbi said that he had two doubts: he did not know whether the Baal Shem Tov said "in fifteen years" or "in fifty years," and also whether "in sixteen" or "in sixty." And I heard from others explicitly "sixty years." I asked Rabbi Aaron of Mezhibuzh, and he told me that the Baal Shem Tov said: "Surely I will be reincarnated in this world, but I will not be as I am now." And the meaning is not known, for he said: "Who is he who will ask the meaning of this thing?"

**Summary:** On the last Sabbath before his passing the Baal Shem Tov said something about a time to come, and those who heard were not sure whether he said fifteen, fifty, sixteen or sixty years, and others said clearly sixty. Rabbi Aaron of Mezhibuzh told that the Baal Shem Tov said he would be reincarnated in the world again, but would not be as he was now, and he did not explain.

## 108 — The Unfit Torah Scroll

I heard from the rabbi that in a certain Torah scroll they would always find a mistake. Every time they corrected it, and even so they found a mistake in it again. They showed the Torah scroll to the Baal Shem Tov, and he said that the scroll had been written with money called *shalder gelt*: that is, people played cards, and gave the master of the house rent for the room, one coin each time. The master of the house collected this money, and with this money he had the Torah scroll written. And so it truly was. And the Baal Shem Tov said that it had no repair, and it would be unfit forever, even if they corrected it a great many times.

**Summary:** In a certain Torah scroll mistakes were found again and again, although they corrected it every time. The Baal Shem Tov said that the scroll had been written with money that a householder collected from card games held in his house, and so it was unfit forever, and had no repair.

## 109 — The Goldsmith's Ring

And a story like this I heard from Rabbi Elimelech: once he came to his neighbor the goldsmith, and saw a ring, and took it in his hand, and at once threw it down, and said that this ring had been bought with money from interest. The goldsmith checked, and found that it was so.

**Summary:** Rabbi Elimelech took a ring in his hand at his neighbor the goldsmith's, and at once threw it down, for he sensed that it had been bought with money from interest. The goldsmith checked, and it turned out to be the truth.

## 110 — Rabbi Matil and the Debts

A story: there was a man in Polonne who did kindnesses for the Baal Shem Tov: he gave him flour and fish on credit. When he was close to death, he asked the Baal Shem Tov to watch over his only son, Rabbi Mordechai, who was called Rabbi Matil. And Rabbi Matil was always going up and down. Once he lost his property, and nothing was left him but his house. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "When they send for you from the nobleman's court to lease the town's *arenda* (the lease), do not go, even several times, until they send a wagon for you." And so it was: several men came to lease the arenda, and they could not agree with them on the price. The lady sent for him, and he did not want to go, and said that he was not well and could not walk. And so it truly was, that he had a slight ailment. And so too on the second day and on the third day, until she sent a wagon for him. He said that he could not hold the arenda, for he had nothing with which to distill vodka. She said to him: "I will give you several hundred measures of grain." And he leased the arenda, and the lady was filled with joy that she had leased it to him. He earned about one thousand two hundred zlotys from it. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Do not trade in oxen." And he did not listen to him, and traded in oxen, and came back from the journey with an empty purse. And so it happened several times.

Once he went down very low, and owed a nobleman about one thousand three hundred zlotys, and had nothing to pay with. The nobleman said: "If he does not pay me, I will kill him with my gun." Rabbi Matil heard and was afraid, and traveled to the Baal Shem Tov, and stayed there all the Days of Awe. The Baal Shem Tov asked him about it, and he said: "I have no money." After Yom Kippur, once before dawn, the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Come with me to the mikveh." He went with him, and afterward he said to him: "Go home." He asked: "What will happen when I go home?" He said to him: "Do not fear and do not be dismayed." And he went home, and celebrated the whole festival.

After the festival there was a time of rains, and from great worry he smoked his pipe, and looked out of the window, and saw a band of gentiles, called Haidamaks, who were coming back from the road. That town was always on their usual route. They went all over the town looking for lodgings, and did not find any, for they were about forty or fifty herdsmen. When they came to his house, he took them in, gave them lodgings, and gave them food and drink, all their hearts desired. He bought two quarts of vodka from his neighbor and sold it to them, and bought more vodka, and earned a lot from them, until he could buy a barrel of vodka. They ate and drank as they wished. And when they went on their way, they asked him to bring barrels of mead after them, and they had a desire to drink all the barrels of mead, and they came back to the town and stayed the night with him. And so it happened several times, until he had the sum of the debt, and he lacked only two more zlotys to complete the debt in full. And one of the herdsmen slipped away from them, came to his house, and counted out the two zlotys for him.

Afterward the nobleman came riding on a horse, and called him to come out to him, and demanded the money from him. He said to him: "I have money." He brought the nobleman into his house, and paid him the whole sum that was owed him. The nobleman said to him: "They told me about you that you had nothing to pay with, and now I see that you have paid me, and the thing was a lie. So take the money back, for as long as you like." And he did not want to take the money from him, for he said: "A miracle does not happen every day." And the Baal Shem Tov said: "How lucky that nobleman is! If you had not had the money, that nobleman would have had to die."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov watched over Rabbi Matil, the son of a man who had helped him, and helped him get a profitable lease, but Rabbi Matil did not listen to his advice not to trade in oxen, and lost again and again. When he came to owe a nobleman a large sum and the nobleman threatened to kill him, the Baal Shem Tov immersed with him in the mikveh and sent him home. There dozens of herdsmen stayed with him and bought drinks from him, until he had earned exactly the sum of the debt, and he paid the nobleman, and the Baal Shem Tov said that thanks to this the nobleman himself was saved from death.

## 111 — Rabbi Gershon's Mishnah and the Enemies

I heard from the rabbi of Nemirov and of Polonne: it is written in the *Zohar Chadash*: "All those sages of the Mishnah and the Talmud, the whole world stands by their merit. When they stood together, a man would say to his fellow: from this Mishnah that has blossomed from his mouth, I see what will be today or tomorrow." And I heard that the Baal Shem Tov, when he heard someone studying, would know what would be at the end of the year. Once I came to Brody, and did not find Rabbi Gershon at home, for he had gone on a journey. Meanwhile Rabbi Gershon came home. Rabbi Gershon's son said to his mother: "I have good news for you: they have taken the factor away in iron chains!" Rabbi Gershon said to him: "Israel, my brother-in-law, said last year that it would be so. For I was studying Mishnah after the prayer, and I studied one Mishnah, and the Baal Shem Tov said to me: 'Do you know what you studied?' I said: 'I know.' And I thought he was saying that I had studied against the plain meaning of the Mishnah. And he said to me: 'You studied two new things: you have two enemies here. One will make peace with you, and the other will have a great downfall.'" And so it was: Rabbi Gershon went to greet the rich men on a festival, and passed in front of the house of one of the enemies, and the enemy sent his son-in-law, and asked him to come into his house, and made peace with him.

**Summary:** It is written in the Zohar that from the Mishnah a tzaddik studies one can see what will happen, and the Baal Shem Tov, when he heard a man studying, knew what would be at the end of the year. When Rabbi Gershon studied a Mishnah, the Baal Shem Tov told him that he had two enemies, and that one would make peace with him and the other would have a great downfall. A year later one of them made peace with Rabbi Gershon, and the other, the factor, was taken away in chains.

## 112 — The Sick Disciple and the Garden of Eden

A story: when the Baal Shem Tov came to Mezhibuzh, he was not important in the eyes of the Hasidim, that is, Rabbi Zev Kutzes and Rabbi David Purkes, because of the name people called him, "Baal Shem Tov," for that name does not suit a tzaddik. They had a fine disciple, and he fell ill. He was a good and righteous man, and his teachers went to visit him. The disciple wanted to call the Baal Shem Tov, and they did not want to. Afterward he grew weaker, and they agreed to call the Baal Shem Tov, and said to the sick man: "Whatever he speaks with you, let us know." When the Baal Shem Tov came to him, he ordered everyone to leave the house. And a boy hid there.

The Baal Shem Tov said that the sick man would die. "But this one thing you have not repaired." And he told him what the thing was, and the sick man admitted it. The sick man said: "I hoped to repair the thing, and what can I do now?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Do not worry. I will see to it that you have no hindrance over this thing, and I will bring you into the Garden of Eden. And do not reveal to anyone what I am speaking with you." And the Baal Shem Tov left him. When his teachers came and asked him what he had spoken with him, he did not want to reveal it. The boy spoke up and told them, and they asked the sick man whether the thing was true, and he said: "It is true, that is what he spoke with me." They were greatly amazed: how does he have knowledge on high, of whom they will bring into the Garden of Eden? They made the sick man give them a handshake that he would reveal to them how he was treated after his death.

When he died, he came to them and revealed to them that they had brought him into the Garden of Eden, and that he had not seen the Baal Shem Tov there, and did not know through whom they had brought him in. "But I had no place in the Garden of Eden. When I sat in one place, another came and pushed me from my place and sat in my place, and so in another place, until they had moved me from all the places. And I wander and roam, and did not know why or wherefore. And I thought in my mind: the Baal Shem Tov promised me to bring me into the Garden of Eden, and I have no place of rest. And it gave me great sorrow.

"Once it happened that the people of my palace went to another palace, and I went with them too. When they reached that palace, I jumped up and sat at the table, and they moved me from my place, until I was standing in front of the table. And I saw the Baal Shem Tov sitting among them, saying words of Torah. He asked them a question, and told the members of the academy to answer it for him, and they could not answer it, and he answered it himself, and they all went off, each to his place. I asked the Baal Shem Tov: 'Why do I have no place in the Garden of Eden?' He said to me: 'Because you gave a handshake and did not keep it.'" And at once, said the dead man, "I came to you in a dream." And he told them the matter, and the question and the answer that the Baal Shem Tov had said in the Garden of Eden.

And right away, that Sabbath, they came to the Baal Shem Tov for the third Sabbath meal, and he said this Torah. When he asked the question, they gave the answer, and he said to them: "I know that the dead man told you." And from that day on they drew close to him. And the rabbi of our community said that they did not bring him into the Garden of Eden itself, but gave him a place near the gates of the Garden of Eden, but every time the Baal Shem Tov had ascents of the soul, on his way back he shone upon him.

**Summary:** At first the Hasidim in Mezhibuzh did not think much of the Baal Shem Tov, but when their sick disciple asked to call him, they finally agreed. The Baal Shem Tov told the disciple that he would die, and promised to bring him into the Garden of Eden although he had not repaired one thing, and asked him not to reveal it, but it came out. After his death the disciple appeared to his teachers and told them that he had no place in the Garden of Eden because he had not kept his promise, and that he had seen the Baal Shem Tov saying Torah in the Garden of Eden, and at the third meal the Baal Shem Tov said exactly that Torah, and told the Hasidim that he knew the dead man had told them. From then on they drew close to him.

## 113 — The Tosafot in Tractate Shevuot

A story: there was a teacher named Rabbi Moshe Keds, and he was great in Torah, and the Baal Shem Tov was not important in his eyes. His custom was to send a messenger to the study house, and only close to "Barchu" did he go in to pray. Once a comment of the Tosafot in Tractate Shevuot was difficult for him, and he struggled with it very much, for about two weeks, and could not reach the true explanation. Once he saw in a dream that he went into a palace, and saw the Baal Shem Tov there, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Why do you not know the explanation of the Tosafot? This is the explanation." He woke from his sleep, and remembered the explanation, and got up and took the Gemara to the moonlight, and looked at the Tosafot, and the explanation was very good. Even so, he said in his heart: "Perhaps it is only a dream, and the Baal Shem Tov knows nothing of it." The Baal Shem Tov invited him to the third Sabbath meal, and said to him: "The explanation of the Tosafot is straight and good." Then he knew that the Baal Shem Tov had told him the explanation, and he drew close to him, and became a great tzaddik.

**Summary:** Rabbi Moshe Keds, a teacher great in Torah who looked down on the Baal Shem Tov, struggled for two weeks with a comment of the Tosafot, and in a dream the Baal Shem Tov explained it to him. In the morning he saw that the explanation was right, but thought that perhaps it was only a dream. At the third meal the Baal Shem Tov told him that the explanation of the Tosafot was straight and good, and Rabbi Moshe drew close to him and became a great tzaddik.

## 114 — The Town of Vinnitsa

I heard from Rabbi Moshe, the son of Rabbi Yakil of Mezhibuzh, who was in Nemirov: when his father came with him to the Baal Shem Tov, to have him blessed, the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Be careful, do not travel to the town of Vinnitsa." Once, when he was traveling, he lost his way, and was near that town, and turned back to another road. And Rabbi Moshe guarded himself all his days not to be in the town of Vinnitsa. When he told me this, he did not have many years left before his death. He was traveling on the road, and a false charge was made against him over his permit, and they took him to the town of Vinnitsa. He begged very much that they not take him to Vinnitsa, and even so they took him, and he was held there, and he died in prison. And this was forty years or more after his wedding.

**Summary:** When Rabbi Moshe was young, the Baal Shem Tov warned him not to travel to the town of Vinnitsa, and he was careful about it all his life. Forty years and more later a false charge was made against him on the road over a permit, and he was taken by force to Vinnitsa, and there he died in prison.

## 115 — The Bridge

I heard from Rabbi Moshe Arbeter that the Baal Shem Tov was his mohel, and ordered that he not be taken over a bridge called a *greblia* (a dam). They kept to this for several years. Once they had to travel to a wedding, and took him with them, and he got a hump.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov, who circumcised Rabbi Moshe, ordered that he not be taken over a certain bridge. For several years they were careful, but once, on a journey to a wedding, they took him with them, and he got a hump.

## 116 — The Father of the Rabbi of Ostroh

I heard a story from the rabbi of our community: the Baal Shem Tov was in Kaminka, and said to Rabbi Baruch: "When the rabbi of Ostroh comes here, tell him in my name that his father, the rabbi the 'Chacham Tzvi,' appeared to me, and asked me to tell his son not to travel to the land of Ukraine. And if he does not believe that I saw him, tell him signs of his father's appearance, and all his movements and his clothes, and what stockings he used to wear." When the rabbi came to Kaminka, Rabbi Baruch told him the Baal Shem Tov's words, and when he heard these signs, he believed. He asked them to tell him some words of Torah they had heard from the Baal Shem Tov, and Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka told him what the Baal Shem Tov had said about Rashi's explanation of the verse "When a ruler sins." (I heard the explanation from the Rabbi of Polonne and forgot it, but surely it is written in his books, and see there.) When the rabbi heard the explanation, it pleased him very much, and he said: "Such an explanation is worthy of being said by the rabbi of Ostroh." And he turned back from his journey, and did not travel to Ukraine. And in his old age he traveled to Ukraine, and died in the town of Fastov.

**Summary:** Through Rabbi Baruch, the Baal Shem Tov told the rabbi of Ostroh that his father, the Chacham Tzvi, had asked him that his son not travel to Ukraine, and added exact signs of his father's appearance and clothes. The rabbi believed, heard a word of Torah from the Baal Shem Tov that pleased him, and turned back. Only in his old age did he travel to Ukraine, and he died there.

## 117 — 248 Limbs and 365 Sinews

There was a sick man whom a great Jewish doctor had given up on, and the sick man could no longer speak. The Baal Shem Tov came to that place, and they called him to the sick man. He said to cook meat broth for him, and at once he would begin to speak. They gave him broth to eat, and the Baal Shem Tov treated him, until he recovered. The doctor asked him: "How did you heal him? I knew that his sinews were ruined, and for these sinews there can be no cure." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "You understood the sick man physically, and I understood the sick man spiritually. For a person has 248 limbs and 365 sinews, and corresponding to them there are 248 positive commandments and 365 negative commandments. When a person blemishes one, Heaven forbid, the limb or the sinew corresponding to it is ruined. And when he breaks many negative commandments, many sinews are ruined, and the blood does not flow in them, and the person is in danger. I spoke with the soul, that it should take repentance upon itself, and it did. And through this all the limbs and sinews were repaired, and I could heal him."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov healed a sick man whom a great Jewish doctor had given up on, and gave him meat broth, and the sick man began to speak and recovered. When the doctor asked how he had healed ruined sinews, the Baal Shem Tov explained that each of the 248 limbs and 365 sinews corresponds to a commandment, and that sins ruin them. He spoke with the sick man's soul, it took repentance upon itself, and so the limbs and sinews were repaired.

## 118 — The Stolen Stockings

There was a man with the Baal Shem Tov named Rabbi Aaron. He was a servant of the Baal Shem Tov, and he married, and did not want to go on being a servant, and asked the Baal Shem Tov to take Rabbi Yaakov as servant. Once Rabbi Yaakov traveled with the Baal Shem Tov, and they stayed the night in a village. They cleared a small house for the Baal Shem Tov next to the tavern, and a pair of stockings had been forgotten there on the rod. When the Baal Shem Tov left there, the servant hid the stockings. While they were still driving through the village, the Baal Shem Tov ordered them to stop, and said to the servant: "You stole something." And the servant denied it. He said to him: "The dog is calling you a thief, and you deny it?" And still he denied it. He said to him: "Where are the stockings?" And he admitted it. The servant told his gentile servant to take the stockings back to the tavern. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Go yourself, and return what you stole." And from then on he never stole anything. And the rabbi of our community heard this from the servant himself, and I heard it from him.

**Summary:** A new servant of the Baal Shem Tov hid a pair of stockings that had been forgotten in the house where they stayed the night. The Baal Shem Tov stopped the wagon and told him he had stolen, and he denied it, until the Baal Shem Tov asked where the stockings were, and then he admitted it. The Baal Shem Tov ordered him to return them himself, and from then on he did not steal again.

## 119 — The Demon's Book

I heard from the rabbi of our community: once a man wrote down the Baal Shem Tov's Torah, what he heard from him. Once the Baal Shem Tov saw a demon walking along, holding a book in its hand. He said to it: "What is this book you are carrying in your hand?" It answered him: "This is the book you wrote." Then the Baal Shem Tov understood that there was a man who was writing down his Torah. He gathered all his people, and asked them: "Which of you is writing down my Torah?" That man admitted it, and brought him the writings. The Baal Shem Tov looked through them, and said: "There is not even one word here that I said."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw a demon carrying a book, and the demon said it was the book the Baal Shem Tov had written. In this way the Baal Shem Tov found out that one of his people was writing down his words of Torah, and when he looked through the writings, he said there was not even one word in them that he had really said.

## 120 — The Silver Vessels

Once the Baal Shem Tov went with his son, Rabbi Tzvi, who was still a boy, to greet the rabbi of the town. He looked and saw many silver vessels there. On the way back he said to his son: "Surely you are jealous: why does your father have no silver vessels?" His son said: "Yes." He said to him: "If your father had money for silver vessels, it would be good to support poor people with it, and to give the rest to charity."

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov saw many silver vessels in the house of the town rabbi, he asked his young son whether he was jealous that his father had no such vessels, and the son admitted it. The Baal Shem Tov explained that if he had money, he would support poor people with it and give the rest to charity.

## 121 — The Money That Never Stayed the Night

And I heard from the rabbi that money never stayed the night with the Baal Shem Tov. When he came back from a journey, he paid debts, and the rest he scattered to charity that same day.

**Summary:** With the Baal Shem Tov money never stayed overnight: when he came back from a journey, he paid debts and gave away all the rest to charity that same day.

## 122 — The Money His Wife Took

Once the Baal Shem Tov brought a lot of money back from a journey. He paid debts, and gave charity, and asked his people to find him a mitzvah on which to spend the money. Meanwhile his wife took some of the money, and said: "This way I will not have to buy on credit for a few days." At night he had a hindrance in his service of God, and he understood. He came into the house and said: "Confess to me: who took from my money?" His wife admitted to him that she had taken from the money, and gave it back. And at night he ordered the poor to be gathered, and gave out the money to them.

**Summary:** Once, after the Baal Shem Tov had paid debts and given charity, his wife secretly took a little of the money that was left, so as not to buy on credit. At night he felt a hindrance in his service of God, and understood that someone had taken from the money. His wife admitted it and gave it back, and he at once gave out the money to the poor.

## 123 — The Jester in the House in Kitov

I heard from Rabbi Gedaliah the rabbi: the daughter-in-law of Rabbi Moshe, the rabbi of Kitov, had sorrow in raising children (her children died), and she sought help from gentile witches. They worked their witchcraft, and buried a capon under the threshold of the house, and through the witchcraft a "jester" (a harmful spirit) came into the house. Once at night Rabbi Moshe saw pigs walking on the table, and he left the house. The Mokhiach came and asked the rabbi: "I will move in to live in your house." He answered him: "If it were a good dwelling, I would live in it myself." The Mokhiach said to him: "Even so, I will live in it," for he had trouble with his neighbors. And he moved in to live in it.

Once the Mokhiach was sitting in his small room, in tallit and tefillin, and the door was open, and his baby was walking in the passageway. The Mokhiach saw the jester going toward the child, and rebuked it and said to it: "What are you doing here?" It answered him: "And what are you doing here?" The Mokhiach understood that he must leave the dwelling, but it was toward evening, and he said: "Tomorrow I will leave here." He asked three friends to stay the night with him. They sat and studied, and afterward the Mokhiach asked to sleep a little, and said to them: "I will go and lie down, and you, please, do not sleep, and study." While he slept, they all fell asleep over the books, and the candle went out, and the jester poured a basket of earth from the attic onto every book.

When the Mokhiach woke and saw that it was dark, he was very frightened. He went into the house, and the jester threw a plank at him from the attic. And had he not moved back a little, into the alcove, he would have been hurt, Heaven forbid. He stood in one place in fear, until he heard the cock crow, and he took a candle and went to light it. In the rabbi's house there was a light, which could be seen through the window, and the Mokhiach went in through the window to light the candle. The rabbi saw that he was very frightened and asked him, and he did not want to answer him until he held fire in his hand, for so it is written in *Sefer Chasidim*. And then he told the rabbi. The rabbi said to him: "I told you so." The Mokhiach went back to his house, and found the friends asleep, and on every book a basket of earth. And in the morning he left the dwelling.

When it was decided that the house would be torn down, the neighbors took from it: this one a bench, and that one a plank, and other wood. And the Mokhiach too took something. And at night the jester knocked on the wall and on the windows of everyone who had taken something from that house. On the third night the Baal Shem Tov said: "I will come to you, and it will not knock in the house anymore. I will lie down to sleep a little, and when you hear it knocking on the window or somewhere else, say: 'Israel son of Eliezer is here,' and it will not knock anymore." And so it was. Then the Mokhiach said: "Since the name 'Israel son of Eliezer' works, it follows that he is a tzaddik." And then he clung to him firmly.

**Summary:** The daughter-in-law of the rabbi of Kitov sought help from witches, and because of witchcraft they buried under the threshold a harmful spirit came into the house, and the rabbi left the house. The Mokhiach moved in to live there, but the spirit went toward his baby, poured earth on his books and threw a plank at him, and the Mokhiach left. Even after the house was torn down, the spirit knocked at night at the homes of everyone who had taken something from it, until the Baal Shem Tov told them to say to it "Israel son of Eliezer is here," and the spirit stopped. From this the Mokhiach understood that the Baal Shem Tov was a tzaddik, and he drew very close to him.

## 124 — The Remedy for Snoring

I heard: once a guest stayed the night with the Baal Shem Tov, and slept in the Baal Shem Tov's house, and he made a sound through his nose, as some people snore in their sleep. Rabbi Tzvi, the Baal Shem Tov's son, was a small child, and was afraid of this sound. He went to his father and told him. His father said to him: "Move the door, as one opens and closes the door, and you will not hear any more sound from him, and he will sleep quietly." He did so, and it helped. On the second night the son went by himself and did this remedy, and it did not help. He went to his father, and his father said to him: "Yesterday, at that moment, the one in charge of sleep was removed from his post, and another was appointed in charge, and in the meantime the remedy helped. And today the new one in charge has already been appointed, and so the remedy did not help."

**Summary:** When a guest snored in the Baal Shem Tov's house and frightened his little son, the Baal Shem Tov told him to move the door, and the snoring stopped. On the second night the son tried again, and it did not help. The Baal Shem Tov explained that on the first night Heaven had been replacing the one in charge of sleep, and during the change the remedy worked, and now it no longer did.

## 125 — The Harmful Spirit That Was Ashamed

A story in Nemirov: there was an unclean house (a house with a harmful spirit in it), and they asked the Baal Shem Tov to go and see the house. The Mokhiach asked to go with him too. The Baal Shem Tov saw a harmful spirit standing there, and the Mokhiach did not see it. The Mokhiach asked, and the Baal Shem Tov said that there was a harmful spirit there. The Mokhiach asked to see it too. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "But you will be afraid." He said to him: "No." He said to him: "Close your eyes and open them several times." He did so, and saw it standing in the corner of the house, with its face to the wall, looking like a completely dark cloud. He asked: "Why has it turned its face away?" The Baal Shem Tov answered: "Because it is ashamed before me." Afterward the Mokhiach began to be afraid, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Close and open your eyes as before, and you will not see it anymore." And he did so, and did not see it anymore. The owner of the house asked the Baal Shem Tov to drive out the harmful spirit, and he did not want to.

**Summary:** In a certain house in Nemirov there was a harmful spirit, and the Baal Shem Tov, who came to see it, showed it to the Mokhiach as well, telling him to close and open his eyes. The spirit stood in the corner with its face to the wall, for it was ashamed before the Baal Shem Tov. When the Mokhiach began to be afraid, he stopped seeing it in the same way, and the Baal Shem Tov did not want to drive the spirit out.

## 126 — The Preacher Who Spoke Ill of Israel

I heard from the rabbi of our community, who heard it from the pious Rabbi Zev of Olyka: once the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath in Olyka, with a householder who was the parnas of the month. On the Sabbath, at the afternoon prayer, the householder went to the synagogue to hear a sermon from a visiting preacher. The Baal Shem Tov waited for him at the third Sabbath meal, to come back from the synagogue. Meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov heard that the preacher was speaking ill of the Jewish people, and he grew angry, and told the attendant to go and call the householder. The attendant revealed to several people that the Baal Shem Tov was angry with the preacher. The preacher saw the people going out one by one, and stopped preaching.

The next day the preacher came to the Baal Shem Tov, and greeted him. The Baal Shem Tov asked him who he was, and he said: "I am the preacher. Why were you angry with me?" The Baal Shem Tov sprang up from his place, and tears burst from his eyes, and he said: "You speak ill of the children of Israel? Know this: a Jew goes all day to the market, and toward evening, as it grows dark, he takes fright and says: 'Woe is me, I will miss the time of the afternoon prayer!' And he goes into a house and prays the afternoon prayer, and does not know what he is saying. And even so, the seraphim and the ofanim (the angels) tremble at it."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov heard from afar a visiting preacher speaking ill of the Jewish people, and grew angry, and the people left the sermon until the preacher stopped. When the preacher came to ask why he had been angry, the Baal Shem Tov sprang up in tears, and told him that a simple Jew, who works all day at the market and in the evening hurries to pray the afternoon prayer without understanding what he says, makes the angels tremble with his prayer.

## 127 — The Servant Who Hauled Wood in Gehinnom

A story I heard from the mouth of Rabbi Moshe, the husband of the sister of the Rabbi of Polonne, and he himself heard it from the Baal Shem Tov, who told it in Nemirov before village people, like this: "Once I saw in a dream that I was walking in a field, and I saw from afar something like a fog. I walked until I reached the place of the fog, on one side. The sun shone on that side and on the road too, and on the other side was the fog. The place was like a long slope, and I walked until I reached the bottom of the valley.

"I had a gentile servant for several years, and he left me. I saw him walking along, with a very great load of wood on his shoulders. When he saw me, he threw the wood off himself, and fell at my feet, and said: 'When I was a servant with your honor, I kept the Sabbath. And when I left you, I worked for a certain tollkeeper, and he made me work on the Sabbath, and ordered me to go to the forest to bring wood on the Sabbath. And now we are both dead, and I must bring wood on the Sabbath day to Gehinnom, until there is enough for the tollkeeper for all the days of the week. So I ask you to wait until I come back, and I will show your honor a place where your honor can ask them to release me from the sentence, for your honor is very important in that world. And now I cannot, because of the overseers who walk behind me.' I said to him: 'If I am important in that world, put down the wood, and come with me at once, and show me the place.' He went with me, and showed me a palace." And the Baal Shem Tov went into it, and spoke in his favor, and they released him from the sentence. "And when I spoke in favor of the gentile, I also pleaded for the Jew, and released him too from the sentence."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov dreamed that he met the gentile servant who had once worked for him, hauling heavy wood in Gehinnom. The servant told him that with the Baal Shem Tov he had kept the Sabbath, but the tollkeeper he worked for afterward made him work on the Sabbath, and now both of them were dead, and he was bringing wood on the Sabbath to the tollkeeper's Gehinnom. The Baal Shem Tov went with him to a palace in Heaven, and spoke in his favor, and they released him from the sentence, and on the same occasion he also pleaded for the Jewish tollkeeper and had him released too.

## 128 — The Servant Who Wanted to Kill

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov was traveling from the village of Chertriye, and stayed the night near the village, by a forest where several Jews had been killed a number of times. He stopped there for the night in order to drive out of that place the *kelipah* of murder. He said to his people: "I want to sleep, and you, do not sleep. Say Psalms, for our gentile servant wants to kill us, and do not sleep all night." In the morning they sent to the village to bring them water to wash, and he drove to the village. When they ate, the Baal Shem Tov called his servant, and gave him bottles of mead, and he drank. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Confess before me that you wanted to kill us in the night." And the servant said: "Yes, it is so."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov stayed the night by a forest where Jews had been murdered, to drive out of there the force of murder, and ordered his people not to sleep and to say Psalms, for their servant wanted to kill them. In the morning he gave the servant drink, and asked him to confess, and the servant confessed that he had wanted to kill them.

## 129 — Again, Near Nemirov

And again the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Once, near Nemirov, you also wanted to kill me." He said to him: "It is true!" He said to him: "Why did you think of it? You know that I cannot be tricked!" The servant said: "As the gentiles say: 'A devil is biting me.'"

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov reminded the servant that once before too, near Nemirov, he had wanted to kill him, and the servant admitted it. When the Baal Shem Tov asked why, since he could not be tricked, the servant answered as the gentiles say: a devil was biting him.

## 130 — The Springs of Wisdom

I heard from the pious Rabbi Yechiel Michel of Zlotchov, who said here, in the town of Linitz, when he was here for the wedding of Rabbi Zev's son, that he was commanded from Heaven to accept the Baal Shem Tov as his master, and to go and learn from him, and they showed him the springs of wisdom that flow to him. And when the Baal Shem Tov passed away, they commanded him to accept the great Maggid, Rabbi Dov, as his master, and showed him that the same springs of wisdom that had flowed to the Baal Shem Tov now flowed to the Maggid.

**Summary:** Rabbi Yechiel Michel of Zlotchov told that he was commanded from Heaven to accept the Baal Shem Tov as his master, and they showed him the springs of wisdom that flow to him. After the Baal Shem Tov's passing they commanded him to accept the Maggid of Mezritch, for the same springs had begun to flow to him.

## 131 — Rabbi Eliezer of Amsterdam in the Land of Israel

I heard from that rabbi that Rabbi Eliezer of Amsterdam traveled to the Holy Land because of Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka, for he said: "When the two of us are in the Holy Land, we will bring the redeemer." When he came to the Holy Land, all the people of the town went out to meet him. When he saw Rabbi Shimshon, Rabbi Nachman's son, he asked him: "Where is your father?" He said to him: "He has traveled abroad." He said: "Oh, oh! I came for him!" And when Rabbi Nachman heard that Rabbi Eliezer had traveled to the Holy Land, he hurried back to the Holy Land, and by the time he reached the Holy Land, Rabbi Eliezer had died, because of our many sins.

**Summary:** Rabbi Eliezer of Amsterdam went up to the Land of Israel to be there together with Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka, for he believed that the two of them together would bring the Messiah. But when he arrived, Rabbi Nachman had already traveled abroad, and when Rabbi Nachman hurried back, Rabbi Eliezer had already passed away.

## 132 — The Informer's Bed

I also heard from the rabbi of our community: there was a rich man in Ludmir, and he had no children. He sent for the Baal Shem Tov. When he came, they prepared a bed for him, and he sat on it. And at once he got up quickly, and said: "On this bed the sin with a married woman was committed." And he said that this bed did not belong to the master of the house: there had been an informer, who did great harm to the people of the town, and when he died, they plundered everything he had, and the master of the house took this bed. The Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath there, and said to the master of the house: "If you agree to have children with defects, you will have children." He agreed, and he had three sons, hunchbacks. <!-- MATURE: a bed on which adultery was committed -->

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov came to a childless rich man in Ludmir, he got up at once from the bed they had prepared for him, for he sensed that a grave sin had been committed on it, and it turned out that it had been taken from an informer's house after his death. The Baal Shem Tov told the rich man that he would have children if he agreed that they would have defects, and he agreed, and three sons were born to him with humps.

## 133 — Abraham, Isaac and Jacob at the Crossroads

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne: two years before the flight, Rabbi Tzvi, the Baal Shem Tov's son, saw his father in a dream. Rabbi Tzvi was in great distress, for he owed money to noblemen, and had nothing to pay with. He asked his father: "Do you know about my distress?" He said: "I know." He asked: "Who told you?" He said: "Those who walk at the crossroads told me." He asked: "Who are those who walk at the crossroads?" He said: "Abraham, Isaac and Jacob walk at the crossroads, to know the troubles of the Jewish people, and they go and make them known at the graves of the tzaddikim. And they told me of your distress. But I have no time to talk with you, for I must go and cancel false accusations by robbers, who are called Haidamaks."

The dream was about three weeks before Passover. And on that festival of Passover they made a false accusation in Sde Lavan, and seized several men, and sent a messenger to the rabbi (of Polonne) to pray for them. On the seventh day of Passover the rabbi said that he went down before the ark with great joy, and at the meal my father-in-law heard him say: "It seems to me that the Shechinah rested on us during the saying of the Song (the Song at the Sea), and I trust in God that the decree will be canceled." And so it was: on the last days of Passover the men came out of prison, and the accusation was completely canceled.

After Passover there were robbers, and they fell upon several villages, and afterward they gathered, until there were about five hundred robbers. At night they fell upon the town of Krasne, and stayed there for a day. The people of the town all fled into the castle, and fought from inside the castle. The gunpowder in the castle had almost run out. The robbers found three women, and took wagons with straw. The women pulled the wagons with the straw, and the robbers pushed from behind the wagons, and they wanted to burn down the castle. At that time a camp of Polish soldiers was stationed near the town of Tuberiv, and at that very moment the Poles arrived with a big cannon, and all the robbers fled, and not one of them was left. And all the summer days it was quiet.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov's son dreamed of his father, and the father said that Abraham, Isaac and Jacob walk at the crossroads, learn of the troubles of the Jewish people and tell the tzaddikim in their graves, and that he was hurrying to cancel false accusations by robbers. On the Passover after the dream a false accusation was made in Sde Lavan, and the Rabbi of Polonne prayed with joy, and the accused were released. Afterward hundreds of robbers attacked the town of Krasne and almost burned the castle, but Polish soldiers arrived with a cannon, and the robbers fled, and it was quiet all summer.

## 134 — Tefillin in the Hour of Danger

I heard from a man named Rabbi Zev, the brother of Rabbi Leib Chasid of Linitz, who settled in the Holy Land for more than thirty years, and passed away in Jerusalem the holy city. Rabbi Zev too was learned and God-fearing, and fit to be believed. And from the rabbi of our community I also heard it in another version, and I will write the rabbi's view of it too.

Once the Baal Shem Tov was very ill, until he had no strength to speak. His people stood by him, and he signaled to them to put tefillin on him. They did so, and he lay in the tefillin a very long time. Afterward he began to speak, and they asked him: "What was that?" He said that a transgression he had committed in his childhood had been found on him, and there was a great accusation against him. "My master came and let me know, and said: 'Quick, put on tefillin!' Afterward the accuser came in the form of a gentile, angry, with iron in his hand, and wanted to cut off my head, and could not come near me because of the tefillin. He shouted at me in Russian: 'Throw off those skins!' And I paid no attention to him, and he shouted until he was undone."

And the rabbi said that the Baal Shem Tov saw that the gates were shut, and they did not want to let his brother-in-law, Rabbi Gershon, come in. And he took a big stick, and beat on the gates until they opened, and went in, and shouted in anger at the court: "For such a small thing you would sentence him to death, Heaven forbid?" And this is what was said in the *Tikkunei HaZohar*: there is a mitzvah called "*Matronita*" (a queen), and she takes a person under her wings, and does not give him into the hand of the accuser, such as the mitzvah of tefillin.

**Summary:** Once the Baal Shem Tov was very ill and lost his speech, and he signaled to his people to put tefillin on him. When he recovered, he told that there had been a great accusation against him over a sin from his childhood, and his master told him to put on tefillin, and the accuser came in the form of a gentile with iron to cut off his head, and could not come near because of the tefillin. According to another version, he saw locked gates that would not let Rabbi Gershon in, and someone broke them open and argued with the court, and in the Zohar it is written that the mitzvah of tefillin protects a person from the accuser.

## 135 — The Judge Who Lost His Torah

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov brought Rabbi Yechiel Michel the rabbi to the rabbinate in the town of Horodna. On Friday night they lost their way. It was very, very dark; truly no one could see his fellow. The Baal Shem Tov got down from the wagon to look for the road, and afterward Rabbi Yechiel Michel the rabbi got down too, and found the Baal Shem Tov lying on the ground, his hands and feet stretched out, weeping bitterly, and tearing the hair of his head, and saying: "They will desecrate the Sabbath, Heaven forbid!" And the rabbi said: "If we had seen nothing in him but this, that he wept bitterly over the desecration of the Sabbath, it would be enough."

Afterward they reached Horodna, and the rabbi preached on the Sabbath, and there was a great debate in the synagogue during the sermon. There was a great scholar there, called Rabbi Michel the judge, and he was very sharp, and raised objections to the rabbi. The rabbi asked him: "At the third Sabbath meal I will answer all your objections." The community had a large house, which they used only when they took on a new rabbi: they made a third Sabbath meal for all the people of the town, and seated each one according to his rank. Rabbi Michel the judge sat beside the rabbi, and Rabbi Zev Kutzes sat at the end of the table. The Baal Shem Tov rebuked him, and said this: "I hate a humble man! Come and sit here."

The rabbi began to go over the sermon, and Rabbi Michel raised objections, and Rabbi Zev debated with him, until he defeated him. And Rabbi Michel was ashamed, and stuck to his opinion although it was crooked, and did not want to admit the truth. The Baal Shem Tov began to plead, and said: "Do not disturb my table," until they calmed down. After the Sabbath the Baal Shem Tov and the rabbi traveled to a circumcision in a village, and Rabbi Michel pushed himself in to sit with them, and they took him. On the way he began to speak of the science of astronomy, and Rabbi Zev Kutzes answered him, and he saw that Rabbi Zev was as learned as he was in astronomy too. He was amazed and said: "How did things like these come to a Hasid?"

When the Baal Shem Tov wanted to go on his way, he was afraid that Rabbi Michel would cause the rabbi grief, and he took his Torah from him, and he became an ignoramus. Rabbi Michel's custom was to get up at midnight to study Gemara, the codes of law and Maimonides. When he opened the book, he knew nothing. He thought his mind was not clear because he had drunk a lot of mead. He closed the book, smoked a pipe, slept a little, and lay until morning. In the morning, after the prayer, he went to study his lesson, took the book and opened it, and knew nothing. And he understood that the Baal Shem Tov had done this to him. He came to the Baal Shem Tov, and said: "Rebbe, give me back my Torah!" And the Baal Shem Tov reproved him and said: "Does one study Torah for the sake of winning and provoking?" And he reproved him, and Rabbi Michel became a complete tzaddik.

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov was bringing Rabbi Yechiel Michel to his rabbinate, he wept and tore his hair on the road when he was afraid they would desecrate the Sabbath. In Horodna a sharp judge named Rabbi Michel raised objections to the new rabbi's sermon, and Rabbi Zev Kutzes, whom the Baal Shem Tov brought up from the end of the table, defeated him, but the judge did not admit it. So that he would not cause the rabbi grief, the Baal Shem Tov took all his learning from him, and when the judge came to ask for it back, the Baal Shem Tov reproved him that one does not study Torah for the sake of winning, and the judge became a complete tzaddik.

## 136 — The Two Princes at War

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne: when there was a war in his days between Greece (Russia) and Ishmael (the Turks), the Baal Shem Tov said: "I saw two princes (angels) fighting, and the prince of Ishmael was winning. And I saw the prince of Greece coming back in fury, and from this a great trouble will come to the Jewish people, Heaven forbid. And I prayed that it would be the other way around, that the prince of Greece would win. Only two Jews I could not save from their hands."

**Summary:** During a war between Russia and Turkey, the Baal Shem Tov saw in Heaven the prince of Turkey defeating the prince of Russia, and understood that from this a great trouble would come to the Jews. He prayed that Russia would win, and thanks to this everyone was saved, except two Jews he could not save.

## 137 — The Candle That Told

I heard that the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath with a certain tzaddik, and the candle began to flicker. The Baal Shem Tov said: "The candle tells me that you were with your wife while the candle was burning." And the truth was so, but the tzaddik was not to blame for this, for the candle had gone out, and he thought it had gone out completely, and afterward the candle began to shine again, like a candle at its end. <!-- MATURE: marital relations by candlelight -->

**Summary:** At a certain tzaddik's house the candle began to flicker, and the Baal Shem Tov said that the candle was revealing to him that the tzaddik had broken a law of modesty by candlelight. The thing was true, but the tzaddik was not to blame, for he had thought the candle had already gone out completely.

## 138 — The Last Zloty in Brody

I heard from Rabbi Zelig of Letichev, who was traveling, and came to the town of Ispali, which was then still a village, and stayed with a shochet. They heard that Rabbi Meir the rabbi, the rabbi of the whole district, had come, and I went to greet him. The rabbi asked Rabbi Zelig to stay there a few days, until all the people of the villages gathered for judgment, and to keep him company. Rabbi Zelig stayed with the rabbi, and the rabbi told before him:

"Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to Brody, and traveled through Horodenka, and stopped in front of my house, and sent to call me to come out. I was not yet head of a rabbinical court then. He said: 'Meir, sit with me in the wagon, and travel with me to Brody.' I took my tallit and tefillin bag and my coat, and went. When we reached Brody, the Baal Shem Tov stopped at an inn, like a merchant. No one came to him, except two men, one of the rich men, and the other not rich. They were important men, and they greeted him, and came to him all the time. And I saw that the money in the Baal Shem Tov's hand had run out, and he did not have even one penny in his hand, and I was sorry: why had I not taken my money from home?

"I heard the Baal Shem Tov say to the wagon driver: 'Get ready for the road.' I went and told those two men that the Baal Shem Tov was leaving, and they came. When they saw that the departure was delayed, they said: 'We will go to the study house, and when he is ready to leave, Rabbi Meir will let us know and we will come.' And so it was: I let them know, and they came. The rich man gave the Baal Shem Tov the first time one gold ducat. And when he came the second time, the Baal Shem Tov took this ducat, and gave it to the rich man, and said: 'Give twelve zlotys to the men of the kloyz,'" for they were important in the Baal Shem Tov's eyes, who said that he saw the Shechinah resting on the kloyz, "'and what is left beyond the twelve zlotys, give out to the poor.' And we drove out of the town.

"When I saw that he had spent this ducat, my heart melted within me, for I knew that he did not have even one penny. I asked him: 'Why do you do this? Your honor does not have even a penny for expenses!' He answered me in plain words: 'Know for certain: as long as God lives, we have nothing to worry about.'" And so it was: when they reached the town of Radvil, the people of the town began to come to him to ask for cures, and so in every place, until he brought money home.

**Summary:** Rabbi Meir the rabbi told that he once traveled with the Baal Shem Tov to Brody, and there, although the Baal Shem Tov did not have a penny left, he gave out to the kloyz and to the poor the only gold coin a rich man had given him. When Rabbi Meir asked him sadly what they would do without money for the road, the Baal Shem Tov answered that as long as God lives there is nothing to worry about. And indeed, in the next town people began to come to him to ask for cures, until he came home with money.

## 139 — The Shochet of the Sect of Shabbetai Tzvi

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov sent word to the rabbi of Bitchov that his shochet was of the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi, may his name be blotted out, and that he was feeding him unfit meat: after he showed the rabbi the knife, he struck the knife on a hammer, to damage it. The rabbi checked on him, and it was so.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov told the rabbi of Bitchov that his shochet belonged to the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi, and that after showing the rabbi a fit knife, he damaged it on purpose and fed him unfit meat. The rabbi checked, and it turned out to be the truth.

## 140 — The Fowl in Konstantin

A story: Rabbi David Purkes came with the Baal Shem Tov to the town of Konstantin. The Baal Shem Tov ordered that the shochet come to show him his knife. The shochet did not come at once, and the Baal Shem Tov stood up to pray. The shochet came, and did not want to wait, and scolded the mistress of the house to give him the birds to slaughter. She gave them, and he slaughtered and left. After the prayer the Baal Shem Tov said: "Has the shochet not come yet?" They answered him: "He already came, and slaughtered the fowl." And the fowl were still lying in the salting. The Baal Shem Tov looked at them, and said that they could be eaten. And Rabbi David did not want to eat, and the Baal Shem Tov did not hold it against him.

**Summary:** In Konstantin the shochet slaughtered fowl without waiting to show his knife to the Baal Shem Tov, who was praying at the time. The Baal Shem Tov looked at the fowl and said that they could be eaten. Rabbi David Purkes did not want to eat, and the Baal Shem Tov did not hold it against him.

## 141 — The Ascent of a Tzaddik's Soul

I heard from the rabbi of our community: once the Baal Shem Tov was in the region of Paberezye, and said that a tzaddik had passed away near where he was. "And it seems to me that he is the authority on Jewish law of Konstantin, for I saw the ascent of his soul, and I recognized his movements."

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov was in the region of Paberezye, he said that a tzaddik had passed away near where he was, and that it seemed to him to be the authority on Jewish law of Konstantin, for he had seen his soul going up and recognized his movements.

## 142 — The Nobleman Who Did Not See the Baal Shem Tov

I heard from the rabbi of our community that a certain nobleman hated the Baal Shem Tov, and said that when he saw the Baal Shem Tov, he would kill him with his gun. Once the Baal Shem Tov was at the castle, and was walking in the castle courtyard with the commissar. That hostile nobleman came to the castle. The commissar left the Baal Shem Tov, went to speak with the nobleman, and brought him into his house. The Baal Shem Tov too went into the house, took leave of the commissar, and went home. The commissar was amazed at this, for he knew that the nobleman was very angry with the Baal Shem Tov. At the meal the commissar spoke with the nobleman about the Baal Shem Tov, and the nobleman grew angry with him, and said again that he would kill him. The commissar said to him: "But he was here when my lord came, and he even came into this house and took leave of me!" The nobleman said: "I did not see him." And he said: "If he is such a man that when he wishes he is not seen, I will make peace with him."

**Summary:** A nobleman who had threatened to kill the Baal Shem Tov came to the castle while the Baal Shem Tov was there with the commissar. The Baal Shem Tov came into the house where they were sitting, took leave of the commissar and left, and the nobleman did not see him at all. When he understood that the Baal Shem Tov could be invisible when he wished, he decided to make peace with him.

## 143 — The Tzitzit That Got Dirty

I heard from the rabbi of our community that once a tzitzit of the Baal Shem Tov's tallit got dirty in the mud. He said: "Rabbi Moshe of Shatanov has died." And he said: "I did not think he was so important that they would record him in my tzitzit."

**Summary:** When a tzitzit of the Baal Shem Tov got dirty in the mud, he understood from it that Rabbi Moshe of Shatanov had passed away. He said that he had not thought Rabbi Moshe was so important that his death would be recorded in his tzitzit.

## 144 — The Rich Man's Wife in Kovel

And by the way I will write what I heard from Rabbi Yechiel the rabbi, head of the rabbinical court of Kovel: in that town there was a man named Rabbi Zusman, and he was very rich, and he was all-powerful in the town. Once on Friday night the rabbi came out from his prayer to go home, and heard that they were still welcoming the Sabbath in the synagogue. He sent to the synagogue to call the shammes, and asked him: "Why are you so late in the synagogue with the welcoming of the Sabbath?" The shammes answered: "Rabbi Zusman was at the castle, and they waited for him." The rabbi said: "And if Rabbi Zusman did not come back until tomorrow, would they not welcome the Sabbath until tomorrow?" Rabbi Zusman's wife heard, and said: "If the rabbi prays in the synagogue, and they do not wait for my husband, for the Kedushah, the Barchu and the Amen, I will close your study house to you!" (For the rabbi prayed in a small special room in the study house, and heard the Kedushah, Barchu and Amen from there.) In the morning, when she got up, she went to the washbasin, washed her face and hands, and went to the towel to dry them, and stood like a silent stone. They ran to the rabbi, and the rabbi was praying, and said "and made the covenant" (in the Verses of Song), and moved his hand, and she died.

**Summary:** In Kovel they delayed the welcoming of the Sabbath until the rich Rabbi Zusman came back, and the rabbi, Rabbi Yechiel, said that the Sabbath cannot be put off for him. The rich man's wife threatened to close the study house where the rabbi prayed, and in the morning, as she was drying her face, she froze like a stone. When they ran to the rabbi, he was praying and said "and made the covenant," and moved his hand, and she died.
